arXiv:2405.12068v2 [math.GR] 12 Jun 2024 
 
 
 Cycles in spherical Deligne complexes and application to  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture for Artin groups 
 
 
 Jingyin Huang
 

 Abstract 
    
 We show the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture holds for Artin groups whose Dynkin diagrams are complete bipartite (edge labels are allowed to be arbitrary), answering a question of J. McCammond. Along the way, we treat several related families of hyperbolic type Artin groups, namely the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture holds for all 3-dimensional hyperbolic type Artin groups, except one single example with Dynkin diagram  [ 3 , 5 , 3 ] [3,5,3] ; and the conjecture holds for all quasi-Lannér hyperbolic type Artin groups up to dimension 4. We also treat several higher dimensional families. 
    
 Most of the article is about developing new methods of understanding combinatorial minimal fillings of certain types of cycles in spherical Deligne complexes or relative Artin complexes, via non-positive curvature geometry. Then we combine this with an approached to the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture introduced by a previous article of the author to settle new cases of  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture. 
    
 In the appendix we list some related open questions and conjectures. 
  
 
 
 1  Introduction 

 
 1.1  Background and overview 

 
 Let  { H λ } λ ∈ Λ \{H_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}  be a locally finite collection of affine complex hyperplanes in  ℂ n \mathbb{C}^{n} . The associated  hyperplane arrangement complement  is defined to the manifold  ℂ n − ∪ λ ∈ Λ H λ \mathbb{C}^{n}-\cup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}H_{\lambda} . Despite the simple definition of such spaces and the long history of studying them, some fundamental aspects of their topology remain elusive; even many basic questions about their fundamental groups are widely open, except for a number of special cases. 
 
 
 One important scenario where these arrangement complements become comparably more
tractable (though still mysterious) is that the collection of hyperplanes has extra symmetry – there is a group acting on  ℂ n \mathbb{C}^{n}  permuting the hyperplanes. As a basic example, we take the family of hyperplanes to be  { z i = z j } 1 ≤ i ≠ j ≤ n \{z_{i}=z_{j}\}_{1\leq i\neq j\leq n}  in  ℂ n \mathbb{C}^{n} . The symmetry group  S n S_{n}  acts on  ℂ n \mathbb{C}^{n}  by permuting the coordinates; hence, it also permutes these hyperplanes. The associated arrangement complement is the configuration space of ordered  n n  points in  ℂ \mathbb{C} , whose fundamental group is the pure braid group on  n n -strands. 
 
 
 The action of the symmetry group in this example is generated by reflections, and the chosen family of hyperplanes are reflection hyperplanes under such action. This has a vast generalization to  any  abstract reflection groups acting on  ℂ n \mathbb{C}^{n} , which gives rise to much more intricate collections of reflection hyperplanes. 
 
 
 Let  Γ \Gamma  be a finite simplicial graph with each edge labeled by an integer  ≥ 2 \geq 2  with its vertex set  S S . The  Artin group (or Artin-Tits group) with presentation graph  Γ \Gamma , denoted  A Γ A_{\Gamma}  or  A S A_{S} , is a group whose generating set is  S S , and there is a relation of the form  a b a ⋯ = b a b ⋯ aba\cdots=bab\cdots  with both sides being alternating words of length  m m 
whenever two vertices  a a  and  b b  are connected by an edge labeled by  m a ​ b m_{ab} . The  Coxeter group with presentation graph  Γ \Gamma , denoted  W Γ W_{\Gamma}  or  W S W_{S} , has the same generating sets and the same relators as the Artin group, with extra relations  v 2 = 1 v^{2}=1  for each vertex  v ∈ Γ v\in\Gamma . 
 
 
 A  reflection  of  W S W_{S}  is a conjugate an element in  S S . Let  R R  be the collection of all reflections in  W S W_{S} .
Recall that  W S W_{S}  admits a canonical representation  ρ : W → G ​ L ​ ( n , ℝ ) \rho:W\to GL(n,\mathbb{R}) , such that each element in  R R  acts as a linear reflection on  ℝ n \mathbb{R}^{n} , and the action  W S ↷ ℝ n W_{S}\curvearrowright\mathbb{R}^{n}  stabilizes an open convex cone  I ⊂ ℝ n I\subset\mathbb{R}^{n} , called the  Tits cone , where the action of  W S W_{S}  is properly discontinuous.
For each reflection  r ∈ W S r\in W_{S} , let  H r H_{r}  be the set of fix points of  ρ ⁡ ( r ) \rho(r)  in  I I . The collection of all such  H r H_{r}  form an arrangement of hyperplanes in  I I .
Define 
 

 
 
 M ( W S ) = ( I × I ) ∖ ( ∪ r ∈ R ( H r × H r ) ) . M(W_{S})=(I\times I)\setminus(\cup_{r\in R}(H_{r}\times H_{r})).\lx@nobreakspace 
 
 
 The  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture for reflection arrangement complements , due to Arnol’d, Brieskorn, Pham, and Thom, predicts that the space  M ⁡ ( W S ) M(W_{S})  is aspherical for any Coxeter group  W S W_{S} . There is an induced action of  W S W_{S}  on  M ⁡ ( W S ) M(W_{S}) , which is free and proper discontinuous. The quotient has its fundamental group isomorphic to the Artin group  A S A_{S} . 
 
 
 Historically, the interests in these topological spaces (i.e., reflection arrangement complements) come from connection with singularity theory: if  W W  is of type  A A ,  D D , or  E E , then  M ⁡ ( W S ) / W S M(W_{S})/W_{S}  is the complement of the discriminant of the semi-universal deformation of a simple singularity of the same type  [ Bri70 ] . More recently, it is known that many of these topological spaces are closely related to  spaces of stability conditions on suitable triangulated categories  [ Bri09 ,  Ike14 ,  QW18 ,  AW22 ,  Hen22 ,  DHL23 ,  QZ23 ] , see also the survey article  [ Hen24 ] . In a few sporadic cases, these topological spaces also arise as components of certain strata of the moduli space of abelian differential in genus 3  [ LM12 ] . 
 
 
 The study of the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1)  conjecture is closely related to the study of Artin groups. Basic questions of Artin groups remain widely open  [ GP12a ] , despite their simple presentation. 
 
 
 To date, the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture is largely open. It is known that the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture for an arbitrary Artin group reduces to the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture for Artin groups with complete presentation graph by the work of Charney-Davis, Ellis-Sköldberg and Godelle-Paris  [ CD95a ,  ES10 ,  GP12b ] . Among Artin groups with a complete presentation graph, the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture is known in the 2-dimension case by Charney-Davis  [ CD95a ] , and a few families that are not exactly 2-dimensional but have strong 2-dimensional features by work of Juhasz and Goldman  [ Juh18 ,  Gol22 ,  Juh23 ] . Known cases of  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture in higher dimensions are sparse - Artin groups associated with finite reflection groups by Deligne  [ Del72 ] , Artin groups with irreduible spherical parabolics being 2-dimensional or  A 3 A_{3}  by Charney  [ Cha04 ] , Artin groups associated with Euclidean reflection groups by Paolini-Salvetti  [ PS21 ]  (based on previous work of McCammond-Sulway  [ MS17 ] ), and a few examples of hyperbolic type Artin groups by Haettel and the author  [ HH23 ] . 
 
 
 In a previous article  [ Hua23 ] , we proved the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture for new families of higher dimensional Artin groups by establishing a connection between the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture and the properties of short cycles in the 1-skeleton of Deligne complexes of spherical Artin groups. This leads to the study of short cycles in spherical Deligne complexes, which is a major challenge in implementing the strategy in  [ Hua23 ]  for more general Artin groups. 
 
 
 In this article and  [ Hua24 ] , we develop new geometric and topological methods to understand certain types of cycles in the spherical Deligne complexes; as a consequence, we settle the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture of several important classes of higher dimensional Artin groups. This article can be read independently, assuming a few facts from  [ Hua23 ] . 
 
 
 The study of cycles in Deligne complexes is closely related to solving certain types of equations over Artin groups. This article focuses on using non-positive curvature geometry to understand these equations, via constructing new actions of certain Artin groups and some of their quotients on injective metric spaces, CAT ( 0 ) (0)  spaces, spaces with convex geodesic bicombing, Helly graphs and weakly modular graphs. The companion article  [ Hua24 ]  explores other methods to understand these equations, including combinatorics of arcs on surfaces, as well as solving equations in free groups. 
 
 
 Some ingredients here will be used in an upcoming article to treat the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1)  problem of some complex hyperplane complements outside the realm of Artin groups. 
 
 
 
 1.2  Main results 

 
 As the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture for general Artin groups is reduced to Artin groups with complete presentation graphs,  our results will be mostly about Artin groups with complete presentation graphs.  It is more convenient to use a different graph in this case. Given an Artin group with presentation graph  Γ \Gamma , its  Dynkin diagram   Λ \Lambda  is obtained from  Γ \Gamma  by removing all open edges of  Γ \Gamma  labeled by  2 2 , and adding extra edges labeled by  ∞ \infty  between vertices of  Γ \Gamma  that are not adjacent. Thus, two vertices of the Dynkin diagram  Λ \Lambda  commute if they are  not  adjacent. For example, the presentation graph of any braid group is a complete graph, but its Dynkin diagram is a line, which is more informative. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.1 . 
 
 (=Theorem  10.8 )

Let  A S A_{S}  be an Artin group such that its Dynkin diagram is complete bipartite. Then  A S A_{S}  satisfies the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture. 
 
 
 
 A  complete bipartite graph  is a join of two discrete sets.
Artin groups with complete bipartite Dynkin diagram were used by McCammond  [ McC17 , Section 8]  to demonstrate how little we understand about Artin groups. When the Dynkin diagram is the star  K 1 , n K_{1,n}  (the join of one vertex and  n n -vertices) and all edges are labeled by  3 3 , the only previously known cases of  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture were  K 1 , 1 K_{1,1}  (type  A 2 A_{2} ) and  K 1 , 2 K_{1,2}  (type  A 3 A_{3} ) in  [ FN62 ] ,  K 1 , 3 K_{1,3}  (type  D 4 D_{4} ) in  [ Bri06 ,  Del72 ]  and  K 1 , 4 K_{1,4}  (type  D ~ 4 \widetilde{D}_{4} ) very recently in  [ PS21 ] . The  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture for the next diagram  K 1 , 5 K_{1,5} , whose Coxeter group acts on the hyperbolic space  ℍ 5 \mathbb{H}^{5}  with finite volume fundamental domain, was already not known before.
The edge labeling in Theorem  1.1  is allowed to be arbitrary. 
 
 
 The choice of the family with complete bipartite Dynkin diagrams is not arbitrary, as it is closely related (in a perhaps less obvious way) to several other natural families of Artin groups. As the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture is known for Artin groups associated with spherical and Euclidean reflection groups  [ Del72 ,  PS21 ] , it is natural to ask the same question for  hyperbolic type Artin groups , i.e., Artin groups associated with Coxeter groups acting on  ℍ n \mathbb{H}^{n}  with finite volume fundamental domain. This was completely understood in the case of  ℍ 2 \mathbb{H}^{2}   [ CD95a ] , with an alternative proof  [ DPS22 ] . However, even in  ℍ 3 \mathbb{H}^{3} , we only know a few sporadic examples of Artin groups satisfying  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture  [ Cha04 ,  HH23 ,  Hua23 ] . 
 
 
 En route to proving Theorem  1.1 , we treat the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture for all 3-dimensional hyperbolic type Artin groups, except a single example. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.2 . 
 
 (=Corollary  9.3 )

Let  W Γ W_{\Gamma}  be a reflection group acting properly on  ℍ 3 \mathbb{H}^{3}  by isometries, with a finite volume fundamental domain. Assume  W Γ W_{\Gamma}  is not  [ 3 , 5 , 3 ] [3,5,3] . Then the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture holds true for the associated Artin group  A Γ A_{\Gamma} . 
 
 
 
 Here  [ 3 , 5 , 3 ] [3,5,3]  denotes the Coxeter group whose Dynkin diagram is a linear graph with three edges, such that consecutive edges are labeled  3 , 5 , 3 3,5,3 . 
 
 
 For general hyperbolic type Artin groups, the most fundamental classes are the Lannér and quasi-Lannér groups. A hyperbolic type Artin group is  Lannér  if the action of the associated Coxeter group has a fundamental domain being a compact simplex, and is  quasi-Lannér  if the associated Coxeter group has a fundamental domain being a non-compact finite volume simplex. A Dynkin diagram is Lannér (or affine or quasi-Lannér) if the associated Coxeter/Artin group satisfies the same property. We use  A Λ A_{\Lambda}  to denote the Artin group with Dynkin diagram  Λ \Lambda . 
 
 
 Theorem 1.3 . 
 
 (=Corollary  11.4 )

Let  A Λ A_{\Lambda}  be a quasi-Lannér hyperbolic type Artin group of dimension  ≤ 4 \leq 4 . Then the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture holds for  A Λ A_{\Lambda} . 
 
 
 
 Theorem  1.3  is a consequence of a more general theorem treating higher dimensional Artin groups, which we will explain now. A key feature of the Dynkin diagram  Λ \Lambda  of a quasi-Lannér group, is that  Λ \Lambda  has a  core , corresponding to the cusps of the action on  ℍ n \mathbb{H}^{n} , which is an affine subdiagram  Λ ′ \Lambda^{\prime} , such that for any vertex  s ∈ Λ ′ s\in\Lambda^{\prime} , each component of  Λ ∖ { s } \Lambda\setminus\{s\}  is either spherical or affine. Axiomatizing this feature on the diagrams leads to much larger classes of Artin groups which are not necessarily hyperbolic type, but share some common geometric features with the hyperbolic type ones. From this viewpoint, each quasi-Lannér diagram gives rise to one or several families of Artin groups containing examples of arbitrarily high dimension (by contrast, hyperbolic type Artin groups only exist up to a certain dimension), and we will organize the remaining results in this way. 
 
 
 Consideration of cores is part of a strategy to treat all Artin groups; see Section  1.3 . 
 
 
 We have treated one case when the core is a cycle in  [ Hua23 ] . Theorem  1.3  relies on treating other types of cores, which leads to the following more general result. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.4 . 
 
 (=Proposition  11.1 )

Given an Artin group  A Λ A_{\Lambda}  with its Dynkin diagram  Λ \Lambda  containing an induced subdiagram  Λ ′ ⊂ Λ \Lambda^{\prime}\subset\Lambda  such that 
 
 1.  
 
 Λ ′ \Lambda^{\prime}  is the Dynkin diagram of a 3-dimensional irreducible affine Coxeter group; 
 
 2.  
 
 for any vertex  s ∈ Λ ′ s\in\Lambda^{\prime} , each component of  Λ ∖ { s } \Lambda\setminus\{s\}  is either spherical, or has type in  { A ~ 3 , B ~ 3 , C ~ n } \{\widetilde{A}_{3},\widetilde{B}_{3},\widetilde{C}_{n}\} . 
 
 
 Then  A Λ A_{\Lambda}  satisfies the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture. 
 
 
 
 To demonstrate Theorem  1.4 , we give seven families of Artin groups, each family containing examples of arbitrary high dimensions whose  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture was not known before. Some of them can be treated directly by Theorem  1.4 , and others require a small variation of Theorem  1.4 . We use the following convention for drawing a Dynkin diagram: when an edge is unlabeled, then its label is assumed to be 3. 
 
 
 Corollary 1.5 . 
 
 (=Corollary  11.3 )
All Artin groups whose Dynkin diagrams belong to Figure  1  satisfy the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture. 
 
 
 
 Figure 1:  Some variations of quasi-Lannér diagrams 
 
 
 Motivated by a few examples of 5-dimensional quasi-Lannér diagrams (see Figure  2  (I), (II)), we study a family of diagrams, called extended stars, which generalize McCammond’s star diagrams  K 1 , n K_{1,n}  mentioned before, see Figure  2  (III). 
 
 
 Figure 2:  Extended star. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.6 . 
 
 (=Theorem  11.5 )

A Dynkin diagram  Λ \Lambda  is called an extended star if 
 
 1.  
 
 Λ \Lambda  is a tree with a vertex  s ∈ Λ s\in\Lambda  such that each edge containing  s s  has label  = 3 =3 ; 
 
 2.  
 
 each component of  Λ ∖ { s } \Lambda\setminus\{s\}  either contains only one vertex, or contains only one edge whose label is  4 4 . 
 
 
 If  Λ \Lambda  is an extended star, then  A Λ A_{\Lambda}  satisfies the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture. 
 
 
 
 Another type of result concerns Artin groups with fewer generators.
Artin groups with  ≤ 3 \leq 3  generators were understood  [ Del72 ,  CD95a ,  DPS22 ] . However, Artin groups with  4 4  generators already contain many examples whose  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1)  were not understood before. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.7 . 
 
 (=Corollary  9.3 )

Let  A S A_{S}  be an Artin group with  ≤ 4 \leq 4  generators. Suppose the Dynkin diagram of  A S A_{S}  is not  [ 3 , 5 , 3 ] [3,5,3] . Then  A S A_{S}  satisfies the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture. 
 
 
 
 One can obtain more general new examples of high dimensional Artin groups satisfying  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1)  by combining the results in this article, and  [ Hua23 ] . Here is a simple example. 
 
 
 Corollary 1.8 . 
 
 (=Corollary  10.9 )

Given an Artin group  A S A_{S}  with a complete presentation graph.
If its Dynkin diagram  Λ \Lambda  is a tree Dynkin diagram with a collection of open edges  E E  with label  ≥ 6 \geq 6  such that each component of  Λ ∖ ( ∪ e ∈ E { e } ) \Lambda\setminus(\cup_{e\in E}\{e\})  is either spherical or a star  K 1 , n K_{1,n} . Then  A Λ A_{\Lambda}  satisfies the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture. 
 
 
 
 We also obtain a corollary regarding the torsion and center of some Artin groups, using  [ JS23 ] , which belongs to fundamental unsolved problems for Artin groups. 
 
 
 Corollary 1.9 . 
 
 Let  Λ \Lambda  be a connected Dynkin diagram satisfying the assumptions in one of the previous theorems. Then  A Λ A_{\Lambda}  is torsion free and it has a trivial center as long as  Λ \Lambda  is not spherical. 
 
 
 
 We end this section by making a summary of all the examples of hyperbolic type Artin groups with known  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture which are either  Lannér  or  quasi-Lannér , combining results from  [ CD95a ,  Cha04 ,  DPS22 ,  HH23 ,  Hua23 ]  as well as this article. 
 
 
 Corollary 1.10 . 
 
 Suppose  Λ \Lambda  is a Lannér or quasi-Lannér Dynkin diagram of hyperbolic type. Then  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture is known for  A Λ A_{\Lambda}  when either  Λ \Lambda  is associated with a reflection group acting on  ℍ n \mathbb{H}^{n}  with  n ≤ 3 n\leq 3  except  Λ = [ 3 , 5 , 3 ] \Lambda=[3,5,3] , or  Λ \Lambda  belongs to one of the following diagrams in Figure  3 . 
 
 
 
 Figure 3:  Diagram with dimension  ≥ 4 \geq 4  with known  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) . 
 
 
 
 1.3  Comment on the proofs 

 
 We will first review the strategy in  [ Hua23 ] , then explain what is new in this article. 
 
 
 Brief recap from the  [ Hua23 ] 

 
 One general principle to prove the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture for Artin group  A S A_{S}  inductively, proposed in  [ CD95a ] , is to construct a suitable contractible complex  X X  where  A S A_{S}  acts with cell-stabilizers being smaller Artin groups whose  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture are known. There are several different possible complexes one can consider.
Recall that the  Artin complex , introduced in  [ CD95a ]  and further studied in  [ GP12b ,  CMV20 ] , is defined as follows. For each  s ∈ S s\in S , let  A s ^ A_{\hat{s}}  be the standard parabolic subgroup generated by  S ∖ { s } S\setminus\{s\} . Let  Δ S \Delta_{S}  be the simplicial complex whose vertex set corresponds to left cosets of  { A s ^ } s ∈ S \{A_{\hat{s}}\}_{s\in S} . Moreover, a collection of vertices spans a simplex if the associated cosets have a nonempty common intersection. The Artin complex is an analogue of  Coxeter complex  in the setting of Artin group. The definition of a Coxeter complex  𝖢 S \mathsf{C}_{S}  of a Coxeter group  W S W_{S}  is almost identical to the Artin complex, except one replaces  A s ^ A_{\hat{s}}  by  W s ^ W_{\hat{s}} , which is the standard parabolic subgroup of  W S W_{S}  generated by  S ∖ { s } S\setminus\{s\} . Each vertex of  𝖢 S \mathsf{C}_{S}  or  Δ S \Delta_{S}  corresponding to a left coset of  W s ^ W_{\hat{s}}  or  A s ^ A_{\hat{s}}  has a  type , which is defined to be  s ^ = S ∖ { s } \hat{s}=S\setminus\{s\} . The following gives an inductive way to prove the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.11 . 
 
 [ GP12b , Theorem 3.1] 

If  Δ S \Delta_{S}  is contractible and each  { A s ^ } s ∈ S \{A_{\hat{s}}\}_{s\in S}  satisfies the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture, then  A S A_{S}  satisfies the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture. 
 
 
 
 Thus, the task is reduced to showing the Artin complex is contractible. Recall that an Artin group is  spherical , if the associated Coxeter group is finite. The associated Coxeter complex is a sphere, and the Artin complex is a union of top-dimensional spheres intersecting each other in an intricate way that is not contractible  [ Del72 ] . However, 
it is known that the Coxeter complex is contractible whenever the associated Coxeter group is not finite. It is natural to conjecture that the Artin complex is also contractible in this case. 
 
 
 The link  lk ⁡ ( v , Δ S ) \operatorname{lk}(v,\Delta_{S})  of a vertex of type  s ^ \hat{s}  turns out to be a copy of the Artin complex  Δ s ^ \Delta_{\hat{s}}  with  s ^ = S ∖ { s } \hat{s}=S\setminus\{s\} . Thus, if  Δ s ^ \Delta_{\hat{s}}  is contractible, then the link of each vertex of type  s ^ \hat{s}  is contractible. So  Δ S \Delta_{S}  can deformation retract onto its subcomplex spanned by vertices whose type is not  s ^ \hat{s} . The first step in our strategy is to keep performing this kind of deformation retraction, until one reaches a “core” where such deformation retraction is not possible, which motivates the following from  [ Hua23 ] . 
 
 
 Definition 1.12 . 
 
 Let  A S A_{S}  be an Artin group with generating set  S S . Let  S ′ ⊂ S S^{\prime}\subset S . The  ( S , S ′ ) (S,S^{\prime}) -relative Artin complex  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}}  is defined to be the induced subcomplex of the Artin complex  Δ S \Delta_{S}  of  A S A_{S}  spanned by vertices of type  s ^ \hat{s}  with  s ∈ S ′ s\in S^{\prime} . 
 
 
 
 An Artin group  A S A_{S}  is  almost spherical  if its presentation graph is complete, it is not spherical, but for each  s ∈ S s\in S ,  A s ^ A_{\hat{s}}  is spherical. A subset  T ⊂ S T\subset S  is  almost spherical  if  A T A_{T}  is almost spherical. Almost spherical Artin groups are classified: they are either affine, or  one of the hyperbolic Lannér types. If  A S A_{S}  is almost spherical, then the link of each vertex is isomorphic to the Artin complex of a spherical Artin group, which is not contractible by previous discussion, thus the deformation retraction procedure does not work for  Δ S \Delta_{S} .
Thus, these Artin complexes and their relative versions serve as our “cores”. 
We conjecture that the cores are contractible. The following conjecture actually implies the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture for all Artin groups  [ Hua23 ] . 
 
 
 Conjecture 1.13   ( [ Hua23 ] ) . 
 
 Suppose  S ′ ⊂ S S^{\prime}\subset S , and  S ′ S^{\prime}  is almost spherical. Suppose the Dynkin diagrams for  S ′ S^{\prime}  and  S S  are connected and do not have  ∞ \infty -labeled edges.
Then  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}}  is contractible. 
 
 
 
 We plan to metrize  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}}  as a “non-positive curved metric space” to show contractibility. 
There is a natural piecewise Euclidean or piecewise hyperbolic metric we can put on  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}}   [ Hua23 ]  (see Section  A ), and the resulting metric space is conjecturally CAT ( 0 ) (0)  or CAT ( − 1 ) (-1) , which gives contractibility. However, the feasibility of proving this conjecture is not clear due to the difficulty of verifying CAT ( 0 ) (0)  for higher dimensional complexes. 
 
 
 A different metric on  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}} , discussed in  [ Hua23 ] , is conjectured to make  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}}  a metric space with convex geodesic bicombing in the sense of  [ DL15 ] , hence contractible. We have a candidate for such metric if  S ′ S^{\prime}  does not belong to the following examples:  { F ~ 4 , E ~ 6 , E ~ 7 , E ~ 8 , [ 3 , 5 , 3 ] , [ 5 , 3 , 3 , 3 ] } \{\widetilde{F}_{4},\widetilde{E}_{6},\widetilde{E}_{7},\widetilde{E}_{8},[3,5,3],[5,3,3,3]\} . See Section  A  for details of the proposed metric in each case. 
 
 
 Combining with the work of Haettel  [ Hae22 ] , in order to verify the alternative metric in Section  A  has the desired property which leads to the contractibility of the complex, it suffices to understand cycles of length  ≤ 6 \leq 6  in the link of each vertex of  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}}  when  dim ( Δ S , S ′ ) ≥ 3 \dim(\Delta_{S,S^{\prime}})\geq 3 , and cycles of length  ≤ 12 \leq 12  in the vertex links of  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}}  when  dim ( Δ S , S ′ ) = 2 \dim(\Delta_{S,S^{\prime}})=2 . 
 
 
 Thus, the core of this strategy is to understand cycles up to a certain length in relative Artin complexes. This turns out to be the most challenging part, as understanding an  n n -cycle in  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}}  is equivalent to understanding the solution set of the equation 
 

 
 
 w 1 w 2 ⋯ w n = 1 w_{1}w_{2}\cdots w_{n}=1 
 
 (1.14) 
 
 in  A S A_{S} , subject to the constraint that each  w i w_{i}  is contained in a suitable parabolic subgroup of  A S A_{S} . Thus the whole plan relies on the feasibility of solving ( 1.14 ), which is not clear at all - even the word problem for most of these Artin groups is open. 
 
 
 Our plan is to use a propagation argument to reduce the study of cycles in general relative Artin complexes to Artin complexes associated with spherical Artin groups. For a vertex  v v  of type  s ^ \hat{s}  in  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}} ,  lk ⁡ ( v , Δ S , S ′ ) ≅ Δ T , T ′ \operatorname{lk}(v,\Delta_{S,S^{\prime}})\cong\Delta_{T,T^{\prime}}  with  T = S ∖ { s } T=S\setminus\{s\}  and  T ′ = S ′ ∖ { s } T^{\prime}=S^{\prime}\setminus\{s\} . If  A T A_{T}  is already spherical, then the reduction is done. If  A T A_{T}  is not spherical, then we can find  T ′′ ⊂ T T^{\prime\prime}\subset T  such that  A T ′′ A_{T^{\prime\prime}}  is almost spherical. If we understand, say, 6-cycles in the vertex links of  Δ T , T ′′ \Delta_{T,T^{\prime\prime}} , then we can conclude that  Δ T , T ′′ \Delta_{T,T^{\prime\prime}}  is non-positively curved in an appropriate sense. While  T ′′ T^{\prime\prime}  might not equal  T ′ T^{\prime} ,  A T A_{T}  acts on both  A T , T ′′ A_{T,T^{\prime\prime}}  and  A T , T ′ A_{T,T^{\prime}} , this allows us to encode a 6-cycle in  Δ T , T ′ \Delta_{T,T^{\prime}}  as six nice subspaces of  Δ T , T ′′ \Delta_{T,T^{\prime\prime}} , and use the non-positive curvature of  Δ T , T ′′ \Delta_{T,T^{\prime\prime}}  to analyze the configuration of these subspaces. This gives potentially a way to use our knowledge of 6-cycles in the vertex links of  Δ T , T ′′ \Delta_{T,T^{\prime\prime}}  associated to a smaller Artin group  A T A_{T}  to understand 6-cycles in the vertex links of  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}}  associated with a larger Artin group  A S A_{S} . We can keep doing this until  A T A_{T}  is spherical.  1 1 
                 1 
                
                
                
              An important, though a bit more technical point - the properties of cycles that propagate properly under this procedure are different from the existing links in the literature (usually one has to require more), however, we expect that it still concerns cycles of the same length. 
 
 
 Now we are reduced to studying cycles of length  ≤ 6 \leq 6  in  Δ S \Delta_{S}  with  S S  spherical, and cycles of length  ≤ 12 \leq 12  in  Δ S \Delta_{S}  with  S S  spherical and  Δ S \Delta_{S}  being 1-dimensional. The later is already understood. The former is still highly non-trivial. 
 
 
 Remark 1.15 . 
 
 In summary, this strategy relies on three major components. First is to understand cycles of length  ≤ 6 \leq 6  in  Δ S \Delta_{S}  with  S S  spherical. The second is to prove certain properties of cycles up to a given length (mostly up to length  6 6 ) survive under the propagation process mentioned above. Third, in order to extend this strategy so that it can treat all Artin groups, we need a theorem reducing the non-positive curvature of  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}}  to understanding cycles in vertex links up to a given length in  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}}  when  S ′ S^{\prime}  belongs to one of the six exceptional cases. Even without the third component, being able to treat the first and second components in full generality will already lead to fairly general results in the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture. 
 
 
 
 
 What is new in this article. 

 
 As explained before, a main challenge in implementing the strategy in  [ Hua23 ]  is to understand the filling of cycles of length  ≤ 6 \leq 6  in the Artin complex  Δ S \Delta_{S}  with the associated Artin group  A S A_{S}  being spherical. Almost half of  [ Hua23 ]  is to understand how  4 4 -cycles in  Δ S \Delta_{S}  can be filled in the complex. This relies on understanding equation ( 1.14 ) in the case  n = 4 n=4 . The main observation was that solving such an equation can be reduced to understanding when two elements in the group commute. However, this completely breaks down in the case  n > 4 n>4 , which makes understanding 5-cycles and 6-cycles in  A S A_{S}  a different story. This is a major obstacle to generalizing  [ Hua23 ] . 
 
 
 A big portion of this article (Section  4  to Section  7 ), as well as the companion article  [ Hua24 ]  is devoted to developing new methods to understand 6-cycles in some Artin complexes of spherical types, providing strong evidence for overcoming the obstacle in the previous paragraph. The case discussed in the companion article  [ Hua24 ]  uses methods of different flavors, hence is separated from here. We are able to treat certain types of 6-cycles in  Δ S \Delta_{S}  with  S S  being of type  F 4 , A n , D n , H 3 F_{4},A_{n},D_{n},H_{3} , where the  D n D_{n}  case is in  [ Hua24 ] . 
 
 
 We introduce a new ingredient in Section  8  on how some of the properties we prove for spherical type Artin complexes propagate to more general Artin complexes in the sense explained before. In Section  9  to Section  11 , we use the results from section  4  to Section  8 , as well as the method in  [ Hua23 ] , to prove all the main theorems. 
Here we only discuss some of the material in Section  4  to Section  8 . 
 
 
 When  A S A_{S}  is spherical, the Artin complex  Δ S \Delta_{S}  is also called the  spherical Deligne complex . It is also named “generalized building” by Deligne  [ Del72 ] , due to its similarity with spherical buildings. These complexes are unions of “apartments” that are round spheres, however, they are not exactly spherical buildings. Nevertheless, this analogy is  fruitful and influential, leading Charney and Davis to conjecture that spherical Deligne complexes are CAT ( 1 ) (1)  with a naturally defined metric, as a way to prove the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture. 
 
 
 On the other hand, the main property we want to establish, is that given a 6-cycle in the 1-skeleton of  Δ S \Delta_{S} , the minimal filling of this 6-cycle in the 2-skeleton of  Δ S \Delta_{S}  is restricted to a few very specific combinatorial configurations. This is a combinatorial problem that is a reminiscent of Bowditch’s characterization of CAT ( 1 ) (1)  in terms of filling curves of length  < 2 ​ π <2\pi   [ Bow95 ] . 
 
 
 These spherical Deligne complexes are more commonly thought of as “spherical objects” and were studied using intuition from spherical geometry. However, in this article, we treat them as “Euclidean objects”. This viewpoint is motivated by certain topological properties of the associated hyperplane arrangement complements.
We show these spherical complexes contain large pieces satisfying fine notions of non-positive curvature, and a large amount of group theoretical information is encoded in these pieces. While the meaning of pieces and the notions of non-positive curvature vary in cases, this is the organizing principle for us to understand filling cycles in the spherical Deligne complexes. The choice of the form of non-positive curvature is quite subtle - it was pointed out to the author by M. Bestvina that there are other contexts (see e.g. curve complexes and arc complexes  [ Web20 ] ) where a given complex satisfies nice form of negative curvature/non-positive curvature, but fails to meet the types of conditions we want to verify. 
 
 
 Now we discuss cases  A n , H 3 A_{n},H_{3}  in more detail on how we find these non-positive curvature subcomplexes, where we actually have control over filling cycles of  any  length in these subcomplexes. Suppose  A S A_{S}  is an Artin group of type  A n A_{n} , with the associated pure Artin group denoted by  P ​ A S PA_{S} .
Then  Δ S / P ​ A S \Delta_{S}/PA_{S}  is a copy of the Coxeter complex  𝖢 S \mathsf{C}_{S} . This gives a folding map  π : Δ S → 𝖢 S \pi:\Delta_{S}\to\mathsf{C}_{S} . Let  H H  be a  wall  in  𝖢 S \mathsf{C}_{S}  (i.e., fix points of a reflection), and let  K H K_{H}  be the maximal subcomplex of  𝖢 S \mathsf{C}_{S}  contained in an open halfspace bounded by  H H . Let  K ~ H = π − 1 ​ ( K H ) \widetilde{K}_{H}=\pi^{-1}(K_{H}) . While  K H K_{H}  has a natural piecewise spherical metric inherited from the round sphere  𝖢 S \mathsf{C}_{S} , we would like to metrize  K H K_{H}  differently. 
 
 
 Consider the Boolean lattice which is the poset made of all subsets of an  n n -element set, ordered under containment. The Hasse diagram of this poset can be identified as the 1-skeleton of an  n n -dimensional unit cube  [ 0 , 1 ] n [0,1]^{n} . 
A simple computation yields that  K H K_{H}  is isomorphic (as a simplicial complex) to the geometric realization of this poset, which corresponds to a simplicial subdivision of  [ 0 , 1 ] n [0,1]^{n}  into orthoschemes. We endow the unit cube with  ℓ ∞ \ell^{\infty} -metric, which gives a metric on  K H K_{H} , and pulls back to a piecewise  ℓ ∞ \ell^{\infty}  metric on  K ~ H \widetilde{K}_{H} . The following is the main point, and follows from Proposition  5.4 . 
 
 
 Proposition 1.16 . 
 
 Each component of  K ~ H \widetilde{K}_{H}  with this metric is an injective metric space. 
 
 
 
 We conjecture that each component of  K ~ H \widetilde{K}_{H}  is CAT ( 0 ) (0)  if we put the  ℓ 2 \ell^{2} -metric on  K H K_{H} , however, this seems much harder to show. This proposition is good enough for the purpose of gaining control on minimal filling of cycles of any length in the 2-skeleton of  K ~ H \widetilde{K}_{H} ; namely, by a simple procedure (see  [ Hae21 ] , also  [ Che00 ,  CCHO14 ] ), one can produce a Helly graph out of  K ~ H \widetilde{K}_{H}  which is closely related to the 1-skeleton of  K ~ H \widetilde{K}_{H} , and control of filling cycles in Helly graphs was obtained in  [ CCHO14 ] . 
 
 
 Note that the subcomplex  K ~ H \widetilde{K}_{H}  depends on the choice of  H H . Different choices of  H H  give a family of subcomplexes that cover  Δ S \Delta_{S} . If we want to understand the minimal filling of a 6-cycle in  Δ S \Delta_{S}  and this 6-cycle happens to be inside one of these subcomplexes, then we are more or less done. However, this is not the case for all 6-cycles, and actually, we do not have a complete understanding of the minimal filling of all 6-cycles in  Δ S \Delta_{S} . However, for certain types of 6-cycles, we do have an extra argument if they are not contained in one of these subcomplexes, and we will come back to this point in a later part of the discussion. 
 
 
 From a topological viewpoint, the complex  K ~ H \widetilde{K}_{H}  arises naturally from the deconing of the associated  A n A_{n} -type arrangement of hyperplanes.
When  Δ S \Delta_{S}  is 2-dimensional, the complex  K ~ H \widetilde{K}_{H}  was considered by Falk in  [ Fal95 ]  for a different purpose, with a different metric. 
Though the description of the complex in Falk’s paper is different as well, it gives the same complex (see Section  2.4 ). Thus, from now on, we will refer to the subcomplex  K ~ H \widetilde{K}_{H}  of  Δ S \Delta_{S}  as the  Falk subcomplex  associated with  H H . 
 
 
 We also record a curious consequence related to the conjecture of Charney and Davis that the spherical Deligne complex is CAT ( 1 ) (1)  with a natural piecewise spherical metric. While our intention was to circumvent this conjecture, we do manage to prove that in the  A n A_{n}  case, suitable subcomplexes of the spherical Deligne complex are CAT ( 1 ) (1) . 
 
 
 Proposition 1.17   (=Proposition  5.7 ) . 
 
 Suppose  Λ \Lambda  is the type  A n A_{n}  diagram with vertex set  S S . Let  S ′ S^{\prime}  be three consecutive vertices in  Λ \Lambda . Then the relative Artin complex  Δ Λ , Λ ′ \Delta_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}  with the induced Moussong metric on  Δ Λ \Delta_{\Lambda}  is CAT ( 1 ) (1) . 
 
 
 
 Now we turn to the case that  A S A_{S}  is of type  H 3 H_{3} , which is the most technical case in this article. Let  π : Δ → 𝖢 S \pi:\Delta\to\mathsf{C}_{S}  and  H H  as before. We can still define  K ~ H \widetilde{K}_{H} , though now we are no longer able to metrize it as an injective metric space, or a CAT ( 0 ) (0)  or conformally CAT ( 0 ) (0)  space. However, we can get around this issue by the following procedure. 
 
 
 Let  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  be a central arrangement of hyperplanes  { H λ } λ ∈ Λ \{H_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}  in  ℝ n \mathbb{R}^{n} , and let  M ( 𝒜 ) = π 1 ( ℂ n − ∪ λ ∈ Λ ( H λ ⊗ ℂ ) ) M(\mathcal{A})=\pi_{1}(\mathbb{C}^{n}-\cup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}(H_{\lambda}\otimes\mathbb{C}))  and  G ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) = π 1 ​ M ​ ( 𝒜 ) G(\mathcal{A})=\pi_{1}M(\mathcal{A}) . There is a  spherical Deligne complex   𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 \mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}  (see Section  2.4 ) with an action  G ↷ 𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 G\curvearrowright\mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}} . The quotient is  S 𝒜 S_{\mathcal{A}} , which is defined to be the unit sphere in  ℝ n \mathbb{R}^{n}  with a cell structure induced by the intersection of this sphere with  the hyperplanes  { H λ } λ ∈ Λ \{H_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda} . Take a hyperplane of  S 𝒜 S_{\mathcal{A}} , one can still define the associated Falk subcomplex of  𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 \mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}  in a similar way. 
 
 
 From now on, we take  𝒜 = { H λ } λ ∈ Λ \mathcal{A}=\{H_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}  to be the collection of reflection hyperplanes in  ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3}  for the canonical action of the type  H 3 H_{3}  Coxeter group on  ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3} , then the associated  G G  is the pure Artin group  P ​ A S PA_{S}  of type  H 3 H_{3} , and  𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 \mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}  is isomorphic to  Δ S \Delta_{S} . 
Denote the standard generators of the Artin groups  A S A_{S}  of type  H 3 H_{3}  by  { a , b , c } \{a,b,c\} , and assume  m a ​ b = 5 m_{ab}=5  and  m b ​ c = 3 m_{bc}=3 . It turns out the hardest type of 6-cycles in  Δ S \Delta_{S}  is a 6-cycle  ω \omega  whose vertices have types alternating between  a ^ \hat{a}  and  c ^ \hat{c} . This corresponds to an equation in  A S A_{S}  of form 
 

 
 
 w a ​ b ​ w b ​ c ​ w a ​ b ′ ​ w b ​ c ′ ​ w a ​ b ′′ ​ w b ​ c ′′ = 1 , w_{ab}w_{bc}w^{\prime}_{ab}w^{\prime}_{bc}w^{\prime\prime}_{ab}w^{\prime\prime}_{bc}=1, 
 
 (1.18) 
 
 where  w a ​ b , w a ​ b ′ w_{ab},w^{\prime}_{ab}  and  w a ​ b ′′ w^{\prime\prime}_{ab}  are (possibly different) words in the subgroup of  A S A_{S}  generated by  a a  and  b b . Similarly, we define  w b ​ c , w b ​ c ′ w_{bc},w^{\prime}_{bc}  and  w b ​ c ′′ w^{\prime\prime}_{bc} . Understanding the minimal filling of  ω \omega  is closely related to gaining control over each of the terms in the above equation. 
 
 
 Let  Λ ′ \Lambda^{\prime}  be a subset of  Λ \Lambda . This gives a sub-arrangement  𝒜 ′ = { H λ } λ ∈ Λ ′ \mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\{H_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{\prime}} . The natural inclusion  M ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) → M ⁡ ( 𝒜 ′ ) M(\mathcal{A})\to M(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})  gives a surjective homomorphism  P ​ A S → G ⁡ ( 𝒜 ′ ) PA_{S}\to G(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}) . The rough plan is as follows: Suppose we want to control the term  w a ​ b w_{ab}  in the above equation. We first choose carefully the subset  Λ ′ \Lambda^{\prime}  such that the quotient  P ​ A S → G ⁡ ( 𝒜 ′ ) PA_{S}\to G(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})  restricts to an injective map on the pure Artin subgroup  P ​ A a ​ b PA_{ab} . This enables us to consider the same equation in the quotient  G ⁡ ( 𝒜 ′ ) G(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})  and the term  w a ​ b w_{ab}  does not disappear under the quotient. Now we look at an appropriate Falk subcomplex of  𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 ′ \mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}} . The hope is that it is now easier to metrize this Falk subcomplex as a non-positive curved metric space (again, one has to choose  Λ ′ \Lambda^{\prime}  carefully for this to happen). Now we can use the non-positive curvature of the Falk subcomplex to fill in 6-cycles in this subcomplex, which can be translated to an estimate of the term  w a ​ b w_{ab} . 
 
 
 There are a few other important points to successfully implement this plan. The first is that when we make the quotient, we not only want the term  w a ​ b w_{ab}  to be more or less unchanged, we also want to make sure that the other terms are still “non-trivial” in an appropriate sense - for example, if most of the other terms disappear under the quotient, then our plan would fail. This places extra constraint on  Λ ′ \Lambda^{\prime} , as a consequence, it makes the task of arranging the non-positive curvature of the Falk subcomplex harder. However, it turns out that we can arrange  Λ ′ \Lambda^{\prime}  such that the Falk subcomplex has a natural graph of spaces structure, and the term we want to control corresponds to the geometric properties of a vertex space of this graph of spaces. So it suffices to metrize the vertex space so it has non-positive curvature, which makes things easier. 
 
 
 In both cases,  A n A_{n}  and  H 3 H_{3} , the above argument only applies to 6-cycles that are contained in a Falk subcomplex, or whose shadows under the above quotienting process are contained in a Falk subcomplex. This can be combined with another ingredient, namely certain types of projections/retractions in  A S A_{S}  and  Δ S \Delta_{S} , to study more general 6-cycles. We can think of ( 1.18 ) as a loop in the Cayley graph of  A S A_{S} , such that each term of the equation corresponds to a subpath of the loop that is contained in a left  A a ​ b A_{ab} -coset or a left  A b ​ c A_{bc} -coset. Suppose the first term  A a ​ b A_{ab}  corresponds to the identity coset  A a ​ b A_{ab}  in  A S A_{S} , and there is a (not necessarily group theoretic) retraction  r : A S → A a ​ b r:A_{S}\to A_{ab}  such that the  r r -image of other cosets are “small” in  A a ​ b A_{ab} , then we will gain control of the term  A a ​ b A_{ab} . 
There are several kinds of retraction  r : A S → A a ​ b r:A_{S}\to A_{ab}  defined in the literature, we use the one in  [ GP12b ,  CP14 ,  BP23 ,  God23 ]  as it is the easiest to carry out a very explicit computation of the retraction image. However, the retraction images of other cosets onto  A a ​ b A_{ab}  are not always small enough, so sometimes such retraction does not give useful information. This is more likely to happen if the 6-cycle is contained in a Falk subcomplex. So our method would treat those types of 6-cycles that are either contained in a Falk subcomplex, or behave well under retraction. 
 
 
 
 A propagation ingredient 

 
 In Section  8 , we use the following idea to understand minimal fillings of  n n -cycles in the relative Artin complex  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}} , where the ambient Artin group  A S A_{S}  is not necessary spherical. The previous discussion relies heavily on the fact that the ambient Artin group is spherical, hence does not apply. When  S ′ ⊂ S S^{\prime}\subset S  is spherical, the complex  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}}  is again a union of “apartments” which are round spheres. However, again, there is a way of making  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}}  “non-positively curved”. For different choices of  S ′ S^{\prime}  in  S S , the complex  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}}  is always quasi-isometric to  Δ S \Delta_{S}  (as long as it is connected). Though for one choice of  S ′ S^{\prime} ,  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}}  could be a union of round spheres; for a different choice  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}}  could be a union of Euclidean spaces or hyperbolic spaces. 
Actually, as long as  S S  is not spherical, there is always a choice of  S ′ ⊂ S S^{\prime}\subset S  such that the latter possibility happens. This gives some hope to use the geometry of one relative Artin complex to study the geometry of another relative Artin complex, though the naive quasi-isometries between them are useless for understanding the fine local combinatorics of minimal filling disks for short cycles. 
 
 
 Given an  n n -cycle in  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}}  with  S ′ S^{\prime}  spherical, under certain extra assumptions, we are able to enlarge  S ′ S^{\prime}  to  S ′′ S^{\prime\prime}  such that  Δ S , S ′′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime\prime}}  can be endowed with an injective metric or a metric with convex geodesic bicombing. Hence, cycles in  Δ S , S ′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime}}  are contained in a non-positive curved space  Δ S , S ′′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime\prime}}  and we can use the appropriate combinatorial counterpart of the non-positive curvature geometry to understand the fine combinatorics of the minimal filling disks of these cycles. Showing  Δ S , S ′′ \Delta_{S,S^{\prime\prime}}  is non-positive curved relies on checking that certain short cycles in the vertex links have minimal fillings in the links with very specific combinatorial types.
However, links of vertices are again relative Artin complexes, with the ambient Artin groups becoming smaller, so this reduces the complexity of the problem. 
 
 
 
 Ending remarks 

 
 The methods discussed here are based on non-positive curvature geometry. In the companion article  [ Hua24 ] , we use different methods to treat certain cycles in the type  D n D_{n}  case.
In this whole discussion, we have tried to avoid the more technical point of exactly what kind of 6-cycles we are considering. We end this discussion by mentioning two conjectures, namely Conjecture  B.1  and Conjecture  B.2 , which are about precisely what types of 6-cycles we hope to understand in Artin complexes of spherical type, and what kind of minimal fillings we would expect. After the conjectures, there is also a summary of exactly what kind of 6-cycles we have treated so far.
The interested reader is invited to Section  A  and Section  B  for a list of conjectures that could potentially extend the results obtained here, as well as some difficulties of the strategy in  [ Hua23 ]  that remain to be resolved. 
 
 
 
 
 1.4  Structure of the article and reading guide 

 
 The article has three parts. Part 1 consists of  Section  2  and Section  3 , and it is a collection of preliminaries. Part 2 consists of Section  4  to Section  7 , where we discuss minimal filling of 6-cycles in different types of spherical Artin complexes. More precisely, Section  4  is about the type  F 4 F_{4}  Artin complex. Section  5  is about a specific type of 6-cycles in type  A n A_{n}  and  D n D_{n}  Artin complexes. Section  6  and Section  7  are about the type  H 3 H_{3}  Artin complex. Part 3 consists of the remaining sections, where we deduce new cases of the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture. 
 
 
 All the sections in Part 2 of the article are independent from each other, apart from that  Section  7  relies on Section  6 . So after reading Part 1, the reader can take any section in Part 2 and start reading without interruption, with the only exception that reading Section  7  requires material from Section  6 . 
 
 
 The main results of Part 2 are Proposition  4.2 , Theorem  5.6 , and Theorem  7.1 .
Alternatively, after reading Part 1, if the reader is willing to take these three main results of Part 2 for granted, then the reader can go to Part 3 directly, to see how these three results imply new cases of the  K ⁡ ( π , 1 ) K(\pi,1) -conjecture. Here is a more detailed description of Part 3. In Section  8  we prove some partial results on how certain properties of 6-cycles in spherical Artin complexes propagate to more general Artin complexes. Section  8  is also independent from Part 2. In Section  9  to Section  11 , we use the results from Part 2 and Section  8  to prove all the main theorems.

 2  Preliminary I: Hyperplane arrangements and related complexes 

 
 2.1  Artin groups 

 
 Let  Γ \Gamma  be a finite simplicial graph with each edge labeled by an integer  ≥ 2 \geq 2 , with its vertex set  S S . The  Artin group (or Artin-Tits group) with presentation graph  Γ \Gamma , denoted  A Γ A_{\Gamma}  or  A S A_{S} , is a group whose generating set is  S S , and there is a relation of the form  a b a ⋯ = b a b ⋯ aba\cdots=bab\cdots  with both sides being alternating words of length  m m  whenever two vertices  a a  and  b b  are connected by an edge labeled by  m a ​ b m_{ab} . Given an Artin group with presentation graph  Γ \Gamma , its  Dynkin diagram   Λ \Lambda  is obtained from  Γ \Gamma  by removing all open edges of  Γ \Gamma  labeled by  2 2 , and add extra edges labeled by  ∞ \infty  between vertices of  Γ \Gamma  which are not adjacent. We will also write  A Γ A_{\Gamma}  as  A Λ A_{\Lambda} . The  Coxeter group with presentation graph  Γ \Gamma  (or with Dynkin diagram  Λ \Lambda ), denoted  W Γ W_{\Gamma}  or  W Λ W_{\Lambda}  or  W S W_{S} , has the same generating sets and the same relators as the Artin group, with extra relations  v 2 = 1 v^{2}=1  for each vertex  v ∈ Γ v\in\Gamma . There is a homomorphism  A Γ → W Γ A_{\Gamma}\to W_{\Gamma} , whose kernel is called the  pure Artin group , and is denoted by  P ​ A Γ PA_{\Gamma} . 
 
 
 There is a set theoretic section  s : W Γ → A Γ s:W_{\Gamma}\to A_{\Gamma}  to the quotient map  A Γ → W Γ A_{\Gamma}\to W_{\Gamma}  defined as follows. Take an element  g ∈ W Γ g\in W_{\Gamma}  and we write  g g  as a word  w w  in the free monoid  S ∗ S^{*}  generated by  S S . We require  w w  is  reduced , i.e. it is a minimal length word in  S ∗ S^{*}  representing  g g . Then  s ⁡ ( g ) s(g)  is defined to be  w w , viewed as an element in  A Γ A_{\Gamma} . This map is well-defined, as two different reduced words in  S ∗ S^{*}  representing the same element of  W Γ W_{\Gamma}  differ by a finite sequence of relators in  A Γ A_{\Gamma}  (cf.  [ Mat64 ] ). 
 
 
 Recall that for any  S ′ ⊂ S S^{\prime}\subset S  generates a subgroup of  A Γ A_{\Gamma}  which is also an Artin group, whose presentation graph is the full subgraph  Γ S ′ \Gamma_{S^{\prime}}  of  Γ \Gamma  spanned by  S ′ S^{\prime}   [ vdL83 ] . This subgroup is called a  standard parabolic subgroup  of type  S ′ S^{\prime} . A  parabolic subgroup  of  A Γ A_{\Gamma}  of type  S ′ S^{\prime}  is a conjugate of a standard parabolic subgroup of type  S ′ S^{\prime} . A parabolic subgroup of  A S A_{S}  is  reducible  if its type  S ′ S^{\prime}  admits a disjoint non-trivial decomposition  S 1 ′ ⊔ S 2 ′ S^{\prime}_{1}\sqcup S^{\prime}_{2}  such that each element in  S 1 ′ S^{\prime}_{1}  commutes with every element in  S 2 ′ S^{\prime}_{2} . If such decomposition does not exist, then the parabolic subgroup is  irreducible . 
 
 
 
 2.2  Real arrangements and their dual polyhedrons 

 
 Recall that a  hyperplane arrangement  in a real vector space  V = ℝ n V=\mathbb{R}^{n}  is a locally finite collection  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  of affine hyperplanes in  V V .
Let  𝒬 ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A})  be the collection of affine subspaces coming from intersections of elements in  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  (note that  V V  is considered an element of  𝒬 ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A})  as it comes from the empty intersection). Note that each point  x x  in  V V  is contained a unique element in  𝒬 ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A})  which is minimal with respect to containment. This element is called the  support  of  x x . A  fan  of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  is a maximal connected subset of  V V  which is made of points with the same support. Each fan is convex and polyhedral. Denote that collection of all fans of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  by  Fan ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \operatorname{Fan}(\mathcal{A}) .
Note that  V V  is a disjoint union of all elements in  Fan ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \operatorname{Fan}(\mathcal{A}) .
For each  U ∈ Fan ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) U\in\operatorname{Fan}(\mathcal{A}) , choose a point  x U x_{U}  in the relative interior of  U U . The partial order on  Fan ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \operatorname{Fan}(\mathcal{A})  is defined by  U 1 < U 2 U_{1}<U_{2}  if  U 1 U_{1}  is contained in the closure of  U 2 U_{2}  and in this case, we also write  x U 1 < x U 2 x_{U_{1}}<x_{U_{2}} . Let  b ​ Σ 𝒜 b\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  be a simplicial complex whose vertices are  { x U } U ∈ Fan ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \{x_{U}\}_{U\in\operatorname{Fan}(\mathcal{A})} , and whose simplices correspond to chains of form  x U 1 < x U 2 < ⋯ < x U k x_{U_{1}}<x_{U_{2}}<\cdots<x_{U_{k}} . There is a piecewise linear embedding of  b ​ Σ 𝒜 b\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  as a subset of  V V . Now we assemble simplicies of  b ​ Σ 𝒜 b\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  to form another cell complex, which we denote by  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} . The closed cells of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  are in one-to-one correspondence with vertices of  b ​ Σ 𝒜 b\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} . We identify the face of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  associated with vertex  x U ∈ b ​ Σ 𝒜 x_{U}\in b\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  with the union of all simplices of  b ​ Σ 𝒜 b\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  corresponding to chains whose smallest element is  x U x_{U} . In this way each vertex of  b ​ Σ 𝒜 b\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  can also be regarded as the barycenter of a face of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} . 
 
 
 Suppose  B ∈ 𝒬 ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) B\in\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A}) . A face  F F  of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  is  dual  to  B B  if  F ∩ B = { b F } F\cap B=\{b_{F}\} , where  b F b_{F}  denotes the barycenter of  F F . Two faces  F 1 F_{1}  and  F 2 F_{2}  of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  are  parallel  if they are dual to the same subspace in  𝒬 ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A}) : we write  F 1 | F 2 F_{1}\parallel F_{2} . Parallel classes of faces of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  are in one-to-one correspondence with elements in  𝒬 ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A}) . For example, the edges dual to a hyperplane in  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  form a parallel class of edges in  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} . 
 
 
 The 1-skeleton of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  is endowed with a path metric  d d  such that each edge has length 1. Given  x , y ∈ Vert ⁡ Σ 𝒜 x,y\in\operatorname{Vert}\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  it turns out that  d ⁡ ( x , y ) d(x,y)  is the number of hyperplanes separating  x x  and  y y  (cf.  [ Del72 , Lemma 1.3] ). 
 
 
 Lemma 2.1 . 
 
 [ Sal87 , Lemma 3] 

Let  x x  be a vertex in  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  and  F F  be a face of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} . Then there exists a unique vertex  x F ∈ F x_{F}\in F  such that  d ⁡ ( x , x F ) ≤ d ⁡ ( x , y ) d(x,x_{F})\leq d(x,y)  for any vertex  y ∈ F y\in F . The vertex  x F x_{F}  is called the  projection  of  x x  to  F F , and is denoted  Proj F ⁡ ( x ) \operatorname{Proj}_{F}(x) . 
 
 
 
 The proof of the following lemma is left to the reader. 
 
 
 Lemma 2.2 . 
 
 Let  Vert ⁡ F \operatorname{Vert}F  be the vertex set of a face  F F  of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} . Let  E E  be another face of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} . Then  Proj E ⁡ ( Vert ⁡ F ) = Vert ⁡ E ′ \operatorname{Proj}_{E}(\operatorname{Vert}F)=\operatorname{Vert}E^{\prime}  for some face  E ′ ⊂ E E^{\prime}\subset E . In this case we write  E ′ = Proj E ⁡ ( F ) E^{\prime}=\operatorname{Proj}_{E}(F) . 
 
 
 
 Definition 2.3 . 
 
 Let  F F  be a fact of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} . Lemma  2.1  gives a map  π : Vert ⁡ Σ 𝒜 → Vert ⁡ F \pi:\operatorname{Vert}\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}\to\operatorname{Vert}F  which extends to a retraction  Π F : Σ 𝒜 → F \Pi_{F}:\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}\to F  as follows. Note that for each face  E E  of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} ,  π ⁡ ( Vert ⁡ E ) \pi(\operatorname{Vert}E)  is the vertex set of a face  E ′ ⊂ F E^{\prime}\subset F . Then we extends  π \pi  to a map  π ′ \pi^{\prime}  from the vertex set of  b ​ Σ 𝒜 b\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  to the vertex set of  b ​ F bF , by sending the barycenter of  E E  to the barycenter of  E ′ E^{\prime} . As  π ′ \pi^{\prime}  map vertices in a simplex to vertices in a simplex, it extends linearly to a map  Π F : b ​ Σ 𝒜 ≅ Σ 𝒜 → b ​ F ≅ F \Pi_{F}:b\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}\cong\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}\to bF\cong F . 
 
 
 
 Now we consider the properties of nearest point sets between two faces. The proof of the following lemma is left to the reader (using Lemma  2.1  and  [ DS87 ] ). 
 
 
 Lemma 2.4 . 
 
 Let  E E  and  F F  be faces of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} . Define 
 

 
 
 X = { x ∈ Vert ⁡ E ∣ d ⁡ ( x , Vert ⁡ F ) = d ⁡ ( Vert ⁡ E , Vert ⁡ F ) } X=\{x\in\operatorname{Vert}E\mid d(x,\operatorname{Vert}F)=d(\operatorname{Vert}E,\operatorname{Vert}F)\} 
 
 
 and 
 

 
 
 Y = { y ∈ Vert ⁡ F ∣ d ⁡ ( y , Vert ⁡ E ) = d ⁡ ( Vert ⁡ E , Vert ⁡ F ) } . Y=\{y\in\operatorname{Vert}F\mid d(y,\operatorname{Vert}E)=d(\operatorname{Vert}E,\operatorname{Vert}F)\}. 
 
 
 Then 
 
 1.  
 
 there are faces  E ′ ⊂ E E^{\prime}\subset E  and  F ′ ⊂ F F^{\prime}\subset F  such that  X = Vert ⁡ E ′ X=\operatorname{Vert}E^{\prime}  and  Y = Vert ⁡ F ′ Y=\operatorname{Vert}F^{\prime} ; 
 
 2.  
 
 Proj E ⁡ ( Vert ⁡ F ) = X \operatorname{Proj}_{E}(\operatorname{Vert}F)=X  and  Proj F ⁡ ( Vert ⁡ E ) = Y \operatorname{Proj}_{F}(\operatorname{Vert}E)=Y ; 
 
 3.  
 
 Proj E | Vert ⁡ F ′ \operatorname{Proj}_{E}|_{\operatorname{Vert}F^{\prime}}  and  Proj F | Vert ⁡ E ′ \operatorname{Proj}_{F}|_{\operatorname{Vert}E^{\prime}}  gives a bijection and its inverse between  E ′ E^{\prime}  and  F ′ F^{\prime} ; 
 
 4.  
 
 if  𝒲 ⁡ ( E ′ ) \mathcal{W}(E^{\prime})  is the collection of hyperplanes in  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  dual to an edge in  E ′ E^{\prime} , then  𝒲 ⁡ ( E ′ ) = 𝒲 ⁡ ( F ′ ) = 𝒲 ⁡ ( E ) ∩ 𝒲 ⁡ ( F ) \mathcal{W}(E^{\prime})=\mathcal{W}(F^{\prime})=\mathcal{W}(E)\cap\mathcal{W}(F) ; 
 
 5.  
 
 if  𝒲 ⁡ ( E ) = 𝒲 ⁡ ( F ) \mathcal{W}(E)=\mathcal{W}(F) , then  E = E ′ E=E^{\prime}  and  F = F ′ F=F^{\prime} . 
 
 
 In the situation of this lemma we will write  E ′ = Proj E ⁡ ( F ) E^{\prime}=\operatorname{Proj}_{E}(F) . 
 
 
 
 In the situation of Lemma  2.4  (5), we will say  E E  and  F F  are  parallel . In this case, the bijection between  Vert ⁡ E \operatorname{Vert}E  and  Vert ⁡ F \operatorname{Vert}F  given by  Proj E | Vert ⁡ F \operatorname{Proj}_{E}|_{\operatorname{Vert}F}  and  Proj F | Vert ⁡ E \operatorname{Proj}_{F}|_{\operatorname{Vert}E}  are called  parallel translation  between  E E  and  F F . 
 
 
 Definition 2.5 . 
 
 Parallel faces  F F  and  F ′ F^{\prime}  of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  are  adjacent  if  F ≠ F ′ F\neq F^{\prime}  and if they are contained in a face  F 0 F_{0}  with  dim ( F 0 ) = dim ( F ) + 1 \dim(F_{0})=\dim(F)+1 . 
 
 
 
 Definition 2.6 . 
 
 Let  B ∈ 𝒬 𝒜 B\in\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}} . Let  F F  and  F ′ F^{\prime}  be two adjacent parallel faces of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  that are dual to  B B . An  elementary  B B -segment , or an  ( F , F ′ ) (F,F^{\prime}) -elementary  B B -segment  is a minimal positive path from a vertex  x ∈ F x\in F  to  x ′ = p ⁡ ( x ) ∈ F ′ x^{\prime}=p(x)\in F^{\prime} , where  p : F → F ′ p:F\to F^{\prime}  is parallel translation. 
 
 
 
 
 2.3  Salvetti complex 

 
 Let  𝒜 , b ​ Σ 𝒜 , Σ 𝒜 \mathcal{A},b\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}},\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  be as before. Let  𝒫 \mathcal{P}  be the poset of faces of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  (under containment), and let  V V  be the vertex set of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} . We now define the  Salvetti complex   Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  associated with  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , as follows.
Consider the set of pairs  ( F , v ) ∈ 𝒫 × V (F,v)\in\mathcal{P}\times V . Define an equivalence relation  ∼ \sim  on this set by 
 

 
 
 ( F , v ) ∼ ( F , v ′ ) ⇔ F = F ′ ​  and  ​ Proj F ⁡ ( v ′ ) = Proj F ⁡ ( v ) . (F,v)\sim(F,v^{\prime})\iff F=F^{\prime}\text{\ and\ }\operatorname{Proj}_{F}(v^{\prime})=\operatorname{Proj}_{F}(v). 
 
 
 Denote the equivalence class of  ( F , v ′ ) (F,v^{\prime})  by  [ F , v ′ ] [F,v^{\prime}]  and let  ℰ ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \mathcal{E}(\mathcal{A})  be the set of equivalence classes. Note that each equivalence class  [ F , v ′ ] [F,v^{\prime}]  contains a unique representative of the form  ( F , v ) (F,v) , with  v ∈ Vert ⁡ F v\in\operatorname{Vert}F . In  [ Sal87 ]  the  Salvetti complex   Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  is defined as the regular CW complex given by taking  Σ 𝒜 × V \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}\times V  (i.e., a disjoint union of copies of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} ) and then identifying faces  F × v F\times v  and  F × v ′ F\times v^{\prime}  whenever  [ F , v ] = [ F , v ′ ] [F,v]=[F,v^{\prime}] , i.e., 
 

 
 
 Σ ^ 𝒜 = ( Σ 𝒜 × V ) / ∼ . \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}=(\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}\times V)/\sim\ . 
 
 (2.7) 
 
 For example, for each edge  F F  of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  with endpoints  v 0 v_{0}  and  v 1 v_{1} , we get two  1 1 -cells  [ F , v 0 ] [F,v_{0}]  and  [ F , v 1 ] [F,v_{1}]  of  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  glued together along their endpoints  [ v 0 , v 0 ] [v_{0},v_{0}]  and  [ v 1 , v 1 ] [v_{1},v_{1}] . So, the  0 0 -skeleton of  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  is equal to the  0 0 -skeleton of  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  while its  1 1 -skeleton is formed from the  1 1 -skeleton of  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  by doubling each edge.
There is a natural map  p : Σ ^ 𝒜 → Σ 𝒜 p:\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}\to\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  defined by ignoring the second coordinate. 
 
 
 For each subcomplex  Y Y  of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} , we write  Y ^ = p − 1 ​ ( Y ) \widehat{Y}=p^{-1}(Y)  and call  Y ^ \widehat{Y}  the subcomplex of  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  associated with  Y Y .
A  standard subcomplex  of  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  is a subcomplex of  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  associated with a face of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} . In other words, if  F ⊂ Σ 𝒜 F\subset\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  is a face, then  F ^ \widehat{F}  is the union of faces of form  F × v F\times v  in  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  with  v v  ranging over vertices in  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} . 
 
 
 Lemma 2.8 . 
 
 Take faces  E E  and  F F  of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} .
If  [ E , v 1 ] = [ E , v 2 ] [E,v_{1}]=[E,v_{2}] , then  [ Proj F ⁡ ( E ) , v 1 ] = [ Proj F ⁡ ( E ) , v 2 ] [\operatorname{Proj}_{F}(E),v_{1}]=[\operatorname{Proj}_{F}(E),v_{2}] . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Note that  [ E , v 1 ] = [ E , v 2 ] [E,v_{1}]=[E,v_{2}]  if and only if for each hyperplane  H ∈ 𝒜 H\in\mathcal{A}  with  H ∩ E ≠ ∅ H\cap E\neq\emptyset , we know  v 1 v_{1}  and  v 2 v_{2}  are in the same side of  H H . Thus for each hyperplane  H ∈ 𝒜 H\in\mathcal{A}  dual to  Proj F ⁡ ( E ) \operatorname{Proj}_{F}(E) ,  v 1 v_{1}  and  v 2 v_{2}  are in the same side of  H H . Now the lemma follows.
∎ 
 
 
 
 The following is a construction originated in  [ GP12b ] . 
 
 
 Definition 2.9 . 
 
 Let  F F  be a face in  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} . Then there is a retraction map  Π F ^ : Σ ^ 𝒜 → F ^ \Pi_{\widehat{F}}:\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}\to\widehat{F}  defined as follows. Recall that  Σ ^ 𝒜 = ( Σ 𝒜 × V ) / ∼ \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}=(\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}\times V)/\sim . For each  v ∈ V v\in V , let  ( Σ 𝒜 ) v (\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}})_{v}  be the union of all faces in  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  of form  E × v E\times v  with  E E  ranging over faces of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} . By Definition  2.3 , there is a retraction  ( Π F ) v : ( Σ 𝒜 ) v → F × v (\Pi_{F})_{v}:(\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}})_{v}\to F\times v  for each  v ∈ V v\in V . It follows from Lemma  2.8  that these maps  { ( Π F ) v } v ∈ V \{(\Pi_{F})_{v}\}_{v\in V}  are compatible in the intersection of their domains. Thus they fit together to define a retraction  Π F ^ : Σ ^ 𝒜 → F ^ \Pi_{\widehat{F}}:\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}\to\widehat{F} . 
 
 
 
 The following is a direct consequence of the definition. 
 
 
 Lemma 2.10 . 
 
 Take faces  E E  and  F F  of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} . Then  Π F ^ ​ ( E ^ ) = Π F ​ ( E ) ^ \Pi_{\widehat{F}}(\widehat{E})=\widehat{\Pi_{F}(E)} . 
 
 
 
 Let  𝒜 ⊗ ℂ \mathcal{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}  be the complexification of  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , which is a collection of affine complex hyperplanes in  ℂ n \mathbb{C}^{n} . Define 
 

 
 
 M ( 𝒜 ⊗ ℂ ) = ℂ n − ∪ H ∈ 𝒜 ( H ⊗ ℂ ) . M(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathbb{C})=\mathbb{C}^{n}-\cup_{H\in\mathcal{A}}(H\otimes\mathbb{C}). 
 
 
 It follows from  [ Sal87 ]  that  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  is homotopic equivalent to  M ⁡ ( 𝒜 ⊗ ℂ ) M(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}) , thus they have isomorphic fundamental groups. 
 
 
 Let  W Γ W_{\Gamma}  be a finite Coxeter group with its canonical representation  ρ : W Γ → G ​ L ​ ( n , ℝ ) \rho:W_{\Gamma}\to GL(n,\mathbb{R})   [ Bou02 ] . Recall a  reflection  in  W Γ W_{\Gamma}  is a conjugate of a standard generator of  W Γ W_{\Gamma} . Each reflection fixes a hyperplane in  ℝ n \mathbb{R}^{n} , which we call a  reflection hyperplane . Let  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  be the collection of all reflection hyperplanes in  ℝ n \mathbb{R}^{n} . The hyperplane arrangement  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  is called the  reflection arrangement  associated with  W Γ W_{\Gamma} . The following facts are standard, see e.g.  [ Par14 ] . 
 
 
 
 1.  
 
 The fundamental group  π 1 ​ ( M ​ ( 𝒜 ⊗ ℂ ) ) \pi_{1}(M(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}))  is isomorphic to the pure Artin group  P ​ A Γ PA_{\Gamma}   [ vdL83 ] ; 
 
 2.  
 
 As the action of  W Γ W_{\Gamma}  permutes elements in  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , there is an induced action  W Γ ↷ M ⁡ ( 𝒜 ⊗ ℂ ) W_{\Gamma}\curvearrowright M(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathbb{C})  and an induced action  W Γ ↷ Σ ^ 𝒜 W_{\Gamma}\curvearrowright\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  which are free. The quotient of each of these two spaces have  π 1 \pi_{1}  isomorphic to  A Γ A_{\Gamma} . 
 
 3.  
 
 The 2-skeleton of  Σ ^ 𝒜 / W Γ \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}/W_{\Gamma}  is isomorphic to the presentation complex of  A Γ A_{\Gamma} . 
 
 
 
 
 Definition 2.11 . 
 
 When  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  is the reflection arrangement associated with a finite Coxeter group  W Γ W_{\Gamma} , we will also write  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  and  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  as  Σ Γ \Sigma_{\Gamma}  and  Σ ^ Γ \widehat{\Sigma}_{\Gamma} . Note that the 1-skeleton of  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  is isomorphic to the Cayley graph of  W Γ W_{\Gamma} , and the 1-skeleton of  Σ Γ \Sigma_{\Gamma}  is isomorphic to the unoriented Cayley graph of  W Γ W_{\Gamma}  (by smashing each double edge of the usual Cayley graph to a single edge). Thus edges of  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  and  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  are labeled by vertices of  Γ \Gamma . Let  K K  be a subset, or an edge path in  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  or  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}} . Then  Supp ⁡ ( K ) \operatorname{Supp}(K)  is defined to the collection of vertices of  Γ \Gamma  which appear as the label of an edge which is contained in  K K . The  type  of a standard subcomplex of  Σ Γ \Sigma_{\Gamma}  or  Σ ^ Γ \widehat{\Sigma}_{\Gamma}  is defined to be the support of this subcomplex. 
 
 
 
 
 2.4  Falk complexes for affine arrangements 

 
 Let  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  be an affine arrangement. Let  D 𝒜 D_{\mathcal{A}}  be the union of all elements in  Fan ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \operatorname{Fan}(\mathcal{A})  which are bounded in  ℂ \mathbb{C} . Note that  D 𝒜 D_{\mathcal{A}}  is polyhedron complex, whose open cells are elements in  Fan ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \operatorname{Fan}(\mathcal{A})  that are contained in  D 𝒜 D_{\mathcal{A}} . A  face  of  D 𝒜 D_{\mathcal{A}}  is defined to be the closure of an open cell of  D 𝒜 D_{\mathcal{A}} . Each face of  D 𝒜 D_{\mathcal{A}}  is a disjoint union of fans. Top dimensional faces in  D 𝒜 D_{\mathcal{A}}  are in 1-1 correspondence with components of  V ∖ ∪ H ∈ 𝒜 H V\setminus\cup_{H\in\mathcal{A}}H  which are bounded. A face  F F  of  D 𝒜 D_{\mathcal{A}}  is  dual  to a face  F ′ F^{\prime}  of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  if the barycenter of  F ′ F^{\prime}  (as defined in the beginning of Section  2.2 ) is contained in the interior of  F F . In this case, we will also say  F ⊂ D 𝒜 F\subset D_{\mathcal{A}}  is dual to the standard subcomplex  F ^ ′ \widehat{F}^{\prime}  of  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}} . 
 
 
 We now define a simplex complex of group structure  𝒰 \mathcal{U}  on  D 𝒜 D_{\mathcal{A}}  as follows. For a face  F F  of  D 𝒜 D_{\mathcal{A}} , the local group at  F F  is defined to be the fundamental group of the standard subcomplex of  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  which is dual to  F F .
The morphisms between the local groups are induced by inclusions of the associated subcomplexes. By Lemma  2.13 , all the morphisms between the local groups are injective, hence  𝒰 \mathcal{U}  is a simple complex of groups. Moreover, it follows from the retraction in Definition  2.9  that each local group injects into  π 1 ​ ( Σ ^ 𝒜 ) \pi_{1}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}) . Thus  𝒰 \mathcal{U}  is developable with  π 1 ​ ( Σ ^ 𝒜 ) = π 1 ​ 𝒰 \pi_{1}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}})=\pi_{1}\mathcal{U} . 
 
 
 Let  𝔻 𝒜 \mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}  be the development complex of  𝒰 \mathcal{U}  (cf.  [ BH99 , Chapter II.12] ). Note that  𝔻 𝒜 \mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}  is simply-connected. The complex  𝔻 𝒜 \mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}  is defined to be the  Falk complex  of the affine arrangement  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , motivated by the work of Falk  [ Fal95 ] .
We now give an alternative description of  𝔻 𝒜 \mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}} . Let  K ~ \widetilde{K}  be the universal cover of  𝔻 𝒜 \mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}} . We start with a disjoint union of a collection  𝒞 \mathcal{C}  of multiple copies of faces of  D 𝒜 D_{\mathcal{A}}  as follows: for each face  F F  of  D 𝒜 D_{\mathcal{A}} , the copies of  F F  in  𝒞 \mathcal{C}  are in 1-1 correspondence with lifts of  F ^ \widehat{F}  in  K ~ \widetilde{K} , where  F ^ \widehat{F}  is dual to  F F . Now we identify an element  F 1 F_{1}  as a face of another element  F 2 F_{2}  of  𝒞 \mathcal{C} , if the subcomplex of  K ~ \widetilde{K}  associated with  F 2 F_{2}  is contained in the subcomplex of  K ~ \widetilde{K}  associated with  F 1 F_{1} . Then we obtained  𝔻 𝒜 \mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}  from  𝒞 \mathcal{C}  after all such identifications. In particular, there is natural map  𝔻 𝒜 → D 𝒜 \mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}\to D_{\mathcal{A}} , coming from quotienting the action of  𝔻 𝒜 \mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}  by the action of  π 1 ​ 𝒰 \pi_{1}\mathcal{U} . 
 
 
 Note that when  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  is a central arrangement, the space  𝔻 𝒜 \mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}  is not very interesting. In such case, there is another complex, called the  spherical Deligne complex , defined as follows. Let  S 𝒜 S_{\mathcal{A}}  be the unit sphere, endowed with the polyhedron complex structure coming the the intersection of the unit sphere with  𝒜 \mathcal{A} . Then there is 1-1 correspondence between open cells in  S 𝒜 S_{\mathcal{A}}  and elements in  Fan ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \operatorname{Fan}(\mathcal{A})  which are not 0-dimensional. A face  F F  of  S 𝒜 S_{\mathcal{A}}  is dual to a face  F ′ F^{\prime}  of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  if the barycenter of  F ′ F^{\prime}  is contained in the fan associated with  F F . In this case, we also say  F F  is dual to the standard subcomplex  F ^ ′ \widehat{F}^{\prime}  of  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}} . This gives a complex of group structure on  S 𝒜 S_{\mathcal{A}}  as before, where the local group on  F F  is the fundamental group of  F ^ ′ \widehat{F}^{\prime} . Then the  spherical Deligne complex  for the central arrangement  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , denoted  𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 \mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}} , is defined to the development complex of this complex of group over  S 𝒜 S_{\mathcal{A}} . This gives a natural map  𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 → S 𝒜 \mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}\to S_{\mathcal{A}} .
The complex  𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 \mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}  also has a similar alternative description in terms of lifts of standard complexes of  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  in the universal cover as in the previous paragraph. 
 
 
 Let  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  be an arbitrary affine arrangement. Let  x x  be a vertex in  D 𝒜 D_{\mathcal{A}} . The  local arrangement of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  at  x x , denoted by  𝒜 x \mathcal{A}_{x} , is defined to be the collection of elements of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  that contain  x x . Note that  𝒜 x \mathcal{A}_{x}  is an central arrangement, and  lk ⁡ ( x , D 𝒜 ) \operatorname{lk}(x,D_{\mathcal{A}})  can be naturally identified as a subcomplex of  S 𝒜 x S_{\mathcal{A}_{x}} . The following is consequence of the description of  𝔻 𝒜 \mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}  and  𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 x \mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}_{x}} . 
 
 
 Lemma 2.12 . 
 
 Let  x ′ ∈ 𝔻 𝒜 x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}  be a vertex which is mapped to  x ∈ D 𝒜 x\in D_{\mathcal{A}}  under  𝔻 𝒜 → D 𝒜 \mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}\to D_{\mathcal{A}} . Let  N N  be the inverse image of  lk ⁡ ( x , D 𝒜 ) \operatorname{lk}(x,D_{\mathcal{A}})  (viewed as a subset of  S 𝒜 x S_{\mathcal{A}_{x}} ) under the map  𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 x → S 𝒜 x \mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}_{x}}\to S_{\mathcal{A}_{x}} . Then  lk ⁡ ( x ′ , 𝔻 𝒜 ) ≅ N \operatorname{lk}(x^{\prime},\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}})\cong N . 
 
 
 
 
 2.5  Collapsing hyperplanes 

 
 Let  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  be an arrangement of affine hyperplanes in  V V . Let  𝒜 ′ ⊂ 𝒜 \mathcal{A}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{A}  be a sub-collection of hyperplanes. Then there is a cellular map  Σ ^ 𝒜 → Σ ^ 𝒜 ′ \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}\to\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}  defined as follows. First we define a map  Σ 𝒜 → Σ 𝒜 ′ \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}\to\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}} . Note that each fan of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  is contained in a unique fan of  𝒜 ′ \mathcal{A}^{\prime} . As vertices of the barycentric subdivision  b ​ Σ 𝒜 b\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  are in one to one correspondence with fans of  𝒜 \mathcal{A} , this gives a map from the vertex set of  b ​ Σ 𝒜 b\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  to the vertex set of  b ​ Σ 𝒜 ′ b\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime} . One readily checks that vertices of a simplex are mapped to vertices of another simplex. So we can extend linearly to obtain a map  c : Σ 𝒜 ≅ b ​ Σ 𝒜 → b ​ Σ 𝒜 ′ ≅ Σ 𝒜 ′ c:\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}\cong b\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}\to b\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}\cong\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}} . Note that 
 
 1.  
 
 c c  maps a face of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  onto a face of  Σ 𝒜 ′ \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}} ; 
 
 2.  
 
 for a face  F ⊂ Σ 𝒜 F\subset\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  and two vertices  v , v ′ v,v^{\prime}  of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  satisfying  Proj F ⁡ ( v ′ ) = Proj F ⁡ ( v ) \operatorname{Proj}_{F}(v^{\prime})=\operatorname{Proj}_{F}(v) , we have  Proj E ⁡ ( c ⁡ ( v ′ ) ) = Proj E ⁡ ( c ⁡ ( v ) ) \operatorname{Proj}_{E}(c(v^{\prime}))=\operatorname{Proj}_{E}(c(v))  where  E = c ⁡ ( F ) E=c(F) . 
 
 
 Thus  c : Σ 𝒜 ≅ b ​ Σ 𝒜 → b ​ Σ 𝒜 ′ ≅ Σ 𝒜 ′ c:\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}\cong b\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}\to b\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}\cong\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}  induces a continuous map  c ^ : Σ ^ 𝒜 → Σ ^ 𝒜 ′ \hat{c}:\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}\to\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}} . 
 
 
 The map  c ^ \hat{c}  restricted to  Σ ^ 𝒜 ( 1 ) \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}^{(1)}  has a more straightforward description.
As each component of  ℝ n − ∪ H ∈ 𝒜 H \mathbb{R}^{n}-\cup_{H\in\mathcal{A}}H  lies in a unique component of  ℝ n − ∪ H ∈ 𝒜 ′ H \mathbb{R}^{n}-\cup_{H\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}H , this gives  c ^ : Σ ^ 𝒜 ( 0 ) → Σ ^ 𝒜 ′ ( 0 ) \hat{c}:\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}^{(0)}\to\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}^{(0)} . Moreover, two adjacent components of  ℝ n − ∪ H ∈ 𝒜 H \mathbb{R}^{n}-\cup_{H\in\mathcal{A}}H  are either contained in the same component of  ℝ n − ∪ H ∈ 𝒜 ′ H \mathbb{R}^{n}-\cup_{H\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}H  or correspond to two adjacent components of  ℝ n − ∪ H ∈ 𝒜 ′ H \mathbb{R}^{n}-\cup_{H\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}H . This gives  c ^ : Σ ^ 𝒜 ( 1 ) → Σ ^ 𝒜 ′ ( 1 ) \hat{c}:\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}^{(1)}\to\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}^{(1)} , where an edge of  Σ ^ 𝒜 ( 1 ) \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}^{(1)}  is collapsed to a single point if its endpoints are sent to the same point of  Σ ^ 𝒜 ′ ( 1 ) \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}^{(1)} . 
 
 
 
 2.6  Some properties of central arrangements 

 
 Let  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  be a finite arrangement of affine hyperplanes in  ℝ n \mathbb{R}^{n} . We say  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  is  central  if the intersection of all hyperplanes in  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  is non-empty, in which case we can assume all hyperplanes in  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  pass through the origin. Now we assume  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  is central.
Take  H ∈ 𝒜 H\in\mathcal{A} . We define the  deconing  of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  with respect to  H H  to an affine hyperplane arrangement in  ℝ n − 1 \mathbb{R}^{n-1}  as follows. Note that the collection  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  of hyperplanes in  ℝ n \mathbb{R}^{n}  give rise to a collection  𝒜 p \mathcal{A}_{p}  of  ℝ ​ ℙ n − 2 \mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n-2}  in  ℝ ​ ℙ n − 1 \mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n-1} . Let  H p ∈ 𝒜 p H_{p}\in\mathcal{A}_{p}  be the copy of  ℝ ​ ℙ n − 2 \mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n-2}  corresponding to  H H . Then  ℝ ​ ℙ n − 1 ∖ H p = ℝ n − 1 \mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\setminus H_{p}=\mathbb{R}^{n-1} , and the intersection of elements in  𝒜 p − { H p } \mathcal{A}_{p}-\{H_{p}\}  with  ℝ n − 1 \mathbb{R}^{n-1}  is a collection  𝒜 H \mathcal{A}_{H}  of affine hyperplanes in  ℝ n − 1 \mathbb{R}^{n-1} . This affine hyperplane arrangement  𝒜 H \mathcal{A}_{H}  in  ℝ n − 1 \mathbb{R}^{n-1}  is defined to be the deconing of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  with respect to  H H . 
 
 
 It is a well-known fact that  M ⁡ ( 𝒜 ⊗ ℂ ) ≅ M ⁡ ( 𝒜 H ⊗ ℂ ) × ℂ ∗ M(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathbb{C})\cong M(\mathcal{A}_{H}\otimes\mathbb{C})\times\mathbb{C}^{*}  where  ℂ ∗ = ℂ − { 0 } \mathbb{C}^{*}=\mathbb{C}-\{0\} , see e.g.  [ OT13 ] . Thus  π 1 ​ M ​ ( 𝒜 ⊗ ℂ ) ≅ π 1 ​ M ​ ( 𝒜 H ⊗ ℂ ) ⊕ ℤ \pi_{1}M(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathbb{C})\cong\pi_{1}M(\mathcal{A}_{H}\otimes\mathbb{C})\oplus\mathbb{Z} . It is also possible to see this isomorphism on the level of Salvetti complex, see the following lemma, where  U ^ H \widehat{U}_{H}  in the lemma is isomorphic to the Salvetti complex of the deconing of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  with respect to  H H . 
 
 
 Lemma 2.13 . 
 
 Let  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  be a central arrangement in  ℝ n \mathbb{R}^{n} . Take a hyperplane  H H  of  𝒜 \mathcal{A} . Let  U H U_{H}  be a maximal subcomplex of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  contained in one side of  H H . Let  U ^ H \widehat{U}_{H}  be the associated subcomplex of  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}} . Then the inclusion  i : U ^ H → Σ ^ 𝒜 i:\widehat{U}_{H}\to\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  is  π 1 \pi_{1} -injective.
