 Let  S 𝒜 ′ S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}  be the subcomplex of  S 𝒜 S_{\mathcal{A}}  made of points with non-negative coordinates. We assume the vertex of  S 𝒜 ′ S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}  in the first octant corresponding to the line  x 1 = x 2 = ⋯ = x n x_{1}=x_{2}=\cdots=x_{n}  is of type  s ^ n \hat{s}_{n} . Let  𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 ′ \mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}  be the inverse image of  S 𝒜 ′ S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}  under the map  𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 → S 𝒜 \mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}\to S_{\mathcal{A}} . Then there is a simplicial isomorphism between  𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 ′ \mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}  and  𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 ′ \mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}  which preserve types of vertices. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 For the proof, we need an alternative description of  𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 \mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}  and  𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 ′ \mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}} , due to Allcock  [ All13 , Theorem 7.3] .
Let  M ( 𝒜 ) = ℂ n ∖ ( ∪ H ∈ 𝒜 H ⊗ ℂ ) M(\mathcal{A})=\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus(\cup_{H\in\mathcal{A}}H\otimes\mathbb{C})  be the complement of complexified arrangement associated with  𝒜 \mathcal{A} . Similarly we define  M ⁡ ( 𝒜 ′ ) M(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}) . We endow  M ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) M(\mathcal{A})  with the Euclidean metric induced from  ℂ n \mathbb{C}^{n} , which makes  M ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) M(\mathcal{A})  an incomplete metric space whose metric completion (denoted  M ¯ ​ ( 𝒜 ) \bar{M}(\mathcal{A}) ) is  ℂ n \mathbb{C}^{n} . Let  M ¯ ℝ ​ ( 𝒜 ) \bar{M}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{A})  be the real part of  M ¯ ​ ( 𝒜 ) ≅ ℂ n \bar{M}(\mathcal{A})\cong\mathbb{C}^{n} .
Let  M ~ ​ ( 𝒜 ) \widetilde{M}(\mathcal{A})  be the universal cover of  M ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) M(\mathcal{A}) , with the induced length metric from  M ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) M(\mathcal{A}) ; and let  M ^ ​ ( 𝒜 ) \widehat{M}(\mathcal{A})  be the metric completion of  M ~ ​ ( 𝒜 ) \widetilde{M}(\mathcal{A}) . Note that the covering map  M ~ ​ ( 𝒜 ) → M ​ ( 𝒜 ) \widetilde{M}(\mathcal{A})\to M(\mathcal{A})  induced a map  p 𝒜 : M ^ ​ ( 𝒜 ) → M ¯ ​ ( 𝒜 ) p_{\mathcal{A}}:\widehat{M}(\mathcal{A})\to\bar{M}(\mathcal{A}) . We view  S 𝒜 S_{\mathcal{A}}  as the unit sphere in  M ¯ ℝ ​ ( 𝒜 ) \bar{M}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{A}) , hence  S 𝒜 S_{\mathcal{A}}  is a subset of  M ¯ ​ ( 𝒜 ) \bar{M}(\mathcal{A}) . Then  𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 \mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}  is isomorphic to  p 𝒜 − 1 ​ ( S 𝒜 ) p^{-1}_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{\mathcal{A}}) . A similar discussion applies if we replace  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  by  𝒜 ′ \mathcal{A}^{\prime} .
There is a finite sheeted covering map  f : M ⁡ ( 𝒜 ′ ) → M ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) f:M(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\to M(\mathcal{A})  induced by 
 

 
 
 ( z 1 , … , z n ) → ( z 1 2 , … , z n 2 ) (z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\to(z^{2}_{1},\ldots,z^{2}_{n}) 
 
 
 see  [ All02 , Section 4] . Thus  M ~ ​ ( 𝒜 ′ ) = M ~ ​ ( 𝒜 ) \widetilde{M}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})=\widetilde{M}(\mathcal{A})  and  M ^ ​ ( 𝒜 ′ ) = M ^ ​ ( 𝒜 ) \widehat{M}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})=\widehat{M}(\mathcal{A}) . This gives a homeomorphism  f ~ : M ~ ​ ( 𝒜 ′ ) → M ~ ​ ( 𝒜 ) \widetilde{f}:\widetilde{M}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\to\widetilde{M}(\mathcal{A})  with the following diagram commutes: 
 

 
 
 M ~ ​ ( 𝒜 ′ ) {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{M}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})} M ~ ​ ( 𝒜 ) {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{M}(\mathcal{A})} M ⁡ ( 𝒜 ′ ) {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})} M ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(\mathcal{A})} f ~ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}} p 𝒜 ′ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}} p 𝒜 \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathcal{A}}} f \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f} 
 
 
 As there is  L > 0 L>0  such that each point in  M ⁡ ( 𝒜 ′ ) M(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})  has an open neighborhood where  f f  restricts to a  L L -biLipschitz map, thus  f ~ \widetilde{f}  is a biLipschitz homeomorphism, hence it extends to a biLipschitz homeomorphism  f ^ : M ^ ​ ( 𝒜 ′ ) → M ^ ​ ( 𝒜 ) \widehat{f}:\widehat{M}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\to\widehat{M}(\mathcal{A})  which fits into
the following commutative diagram: 
 

 
 
 M ^ ​ ( 𝒜 ′ ) {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{M}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})} M ^ ​ ( 𝒜 ) {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{M}(\mathcal{A})} M ¯ ​ ( 𝒜 ′ ) {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{M}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})} M ¯ ​ ( 𝒜 ) {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{M}(\mathcal{A})} f ^ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{f}} p 𝒜 ′ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}} p 𝒜 \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathcal{A}}} f ¯ \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{f}} 
 
 
 Let  K = f ¯ ​ ( S 𝒜 ′ ) K=\bar{f}(S_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}) . As each point in  K K  has non-negative real coordinates,  S 𝒜 ′ = f ¯ − 1 ​ ( K ) S_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}=\bar{f}^{-1}(K) . Then the above commutative diagram implies that  f ^ \widehat{f}  induces a homeomorphism  p 𝒜 ′ − 1 ​ ( S 𝒜 ′ ) → p 𝒜 − 1 ​ ( K ) p^{-1}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(S_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}})\to p^{-1}_{\mathcal{A}}(K) . Now we consider the map  α : M ¯ ℝ ​ ( 𝒜 ) − { 0 } → S 𝒜 \alpha:\bar{M}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{A})-\{0\}\to S_{\mathcal{A}}  by sending  x x  to  x ‖ x ‖ \frac{x}{||x||} . Note that  α \alpha  induces a homeomorphism  α | K : K → S ′ 𝒜 \alpha_{|K}:K\to S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}} , which gives a homeomorphism  p 𝒜 − 1 ​ ( K ) → p 𝒜 − 1 ​ ( S 𝒜 ′ ) p^{-1}_{\mathcal{A}}(K)\to p^{-1}_{\mathcal{A}}(S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}) . Thus 
 

 
 
 𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 ′ ≅ p 𝒜 ′ − 1 ​ ( S 𝒜 ′ ) ≅ p 𝒜 − 1 ​ ( K ) ≅ p 𝒜 − 1 ​ ( S 𝒜 ′ ) ≅ 𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 ′ . \mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}\cong p^{-1}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(S_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}})\cong p^{-1}_{\mathcal{A}}(K)\cong p^{-1}_{\mathcal{A}}(S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}})\cong\mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}. 
 
 
 One readily verifies that the type of vertices are preserved, as  f ¯ \bar{f}  is type-preserving.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Theorem 5.6 . 
 
 Suppose  Λ \Lambda  is a Dynkin diagram of type  A n A_{n} . Let  Λ ′ ⊂ Λ \Lambda^{\prime}\subset\Lambda  be a linear subgraph made of three vertices. Then  Δ Λ , Λ ′ \Delta_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}  is weakly flag. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 In the following proof, we assume  V ​ Δ Λ V\Delta_{\Lambda}  is endowed with the order induced from  s 1 < s 2 < ⋯ < s n s_{1}<s_{2}<\cdots<s_{n} . Assume vertices of  Λ ′ \Lambda^{\prime}  are  t 1 = s i , t 2 = s i + 1 t_{1}=s_{i},t_{2}=s_{i+1}  and  t 3 = s i + 3 t_{3}=s_{i+3} . We will only prove upward weakly flagness here, as the proof of downward weakly flagness is almost identical. Take vertices  { x i } i = 1 3 \{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{3}  in  Δ Λ , Λ ′ \Delta_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}  of type  t ^ 1 \hat{t}_{1}  and  { y i } i = 1 3 \{y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{3}  of type  t ^ 2 \hat{t}_{2} . Suppose  y i y_{i}  is a common upper bound for  x i x_{i}  and  x i + 1 x_{i+1} . Then we need to show  { x 1 , x 2 , x 3 } \{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\}  have a common upper bound in  Δ Λ , Λ ′ \Delta_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}} . 
 
 
 Let  ω \omega  be the 6-cycle  x 1 ​ y 1 ​ x 2 ​ y 2 ​ x 3 ​ y 3 x_{1}y_{1}x_{2}y_{2}x_{3}y_{3}  in  Δ Λ , Λ ′ \Delta_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}} . Let  π : Δ Λ → 𝖢 Λ ≅ S 𝒜 \pi:\Delta_{\Lambda}\to\mathsf{C}_{\Lambda}\cong S_{\mathcal{A}}  be the map induced by quotienting  Δ Λ \Delta_{\Lambda}  by the action of the pure Artin group. By  [ Hua24 , Proposition 3.1] , it suffices to consider the case when  π ⁡ ( ω ) \pi(\omega)  is a single edge. 
 
 
 We can assume without loss of generality that  π ⁡ ( ω ) ⊂ D ℬ ⊂ S 𝒜 \pi(\omega)\subset D_{\mathcal{B}}\subset S_{\mathcal{A}} . As  𝔻 ℬ \mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{B}}  can be identified with subcomplex of  𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 ≅ Δ Λ \mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}\cong\Delta_{\Lambda}  which is the inverse image of  D ℬ D_{\mathcal{B}}  under the map  𝕊 ​ 𝔻 𝒜 → S 𝒜 \mathbb{S}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}\to S_{\mathcal{A}} , we know  ω ⊂ 𝔻 ℬ \omega\subset\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{B}} . By Proposition  5.4  and Lemma  5.1 , there is a vertex  z ∈ 𝔻 ℬ z\in\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{B}}  which is the common upper bound for  { x 1 , x 2 , x 3 } \{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\}  in  ( V ​ 𝔻 ℬ , ≤ ) (V\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{B}},\leq) . As  ( V ​ 𝔻 ℬ , ≤ ) (V\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{B}},\leq)  is bowtie free, we know  y i y_{i}  is the join of  x i x_{i}  and  x i + 1 x_{i+1} . Thus  y i < z y_{i}<z  in  ( V ​ 𝔻 ℬ , ≤ ) (V\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{B}},\leq) . Then  z z  is of type  s j s_{j}  with  j ≥ i + 2 j\geq i+2 . If  j = i + 2 j=i+2 , then  z ∈ Δ Λ , Λ ′ z\in\Delta_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}  and we are done. Now assume  j > i + 2 j>i+2 . Let  lk − ⁡ ( z , Δ Λ ) \operatorname{lk}^{-}(z,\Delta_{\Lambda})  be the full subcomplex of  lk ⁡ ( z , Δ Λ ) \operatorname{lk}(z,\Delta_{\Lambda})  spanned by vertices  < z <z . Then  ω ⊂ lk − ⁡ ( z , Δ Λ ) \omega\subset\operatorname{lk}^{-}(z,\Delta_{\Lambda}) . Note that  lk − ⁡ ( z , Δ Λ ) ≅ Δ Λ j \operatorname{lk}^{-}(z,\Delta_{\Lambda})\cong\Delta_{\Lambda_{j}}  where  Λ j \Lambda_{j}  is a Dynkin diagram of type  A j − 1 A_{j-1} . By the same argument as before, we find  z ′ ∈ lk − ⁡ ( z , Δ Λ ) z^{\prime}\in\operatorname{lk}^{-}(z,\Delta_{\Lambda})  such that  ω ⊂ lk − ⁡ ( z ′ , Δ Λ ) \omega\subset\operatorname{lk}^{-}(z^{\prime},\Delta_{\Lambda}) . Note that  z ′ < z z^{\prime}<z . Repeating this procedure finitely many times will eventually give  z 0 ∈ Δ Λ z_{0}\in\Delta_{\Lambda}  of type  t ^ 3 \hat{t}_{3}  such that  ω ⊂ lk − ⁡ ( z 0 , Δ Λ ) \omega\subset\operatorname{lk}^{-}(z_{0},\Delta_{\Lambda}) . Then  z 0 ∈ Δ Λ , Λ ′ z_{0}\in\Delta_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}} , as desired.
∎ 
 
 
 
 The  n = 3 n=3  case of Theorem  5.6  was previously known  [ Cha04 , Lemma 4.2] . 
 
 
 By Theorem  5.6 , Theorem  3.17  and  [ Gol23 , Theorem 5.2] , we have the following. 
 
 
 Proposition 5.7 . 
 
 Suppose  Λ \Lambda  is a Dynkin diagram of type  A n A_{n} . Let  Λ ′ ⊂ Λ \Lambda^{\prime}\subset\Lambda  be a linear subgraph made of three vertices. Then  Δ Λ , Λ ′ \Delta_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}  with the induced Moussong metric from  Δ Λ \Delta_{\Lambda}  is CAT ( 1 ) (1) . 
 
 
 
 
 
 6  Some sub-arrangements of the  H 3 H_{3} -arrangement 

 
 This section is a preparation of Section  7 . More precisely, we discuss two sub-arrangements of the  H 3 H_{3} -arrangements and prove some useful properties on the structure of their Falk complexes, which will be used in Section  7 . The reader can start with Section  7 , and refer back to this section if necessary. 
 
 
 6.1  Auxiliary arrangement I 

 
 Let  W Γ W_{\Gamma}  be the Coxeter group of type  H 3 H_{3} . Suppose  Γ \Gamma  has vertex set  { a , b , c } \{a,b,c\} , with  m a ​ b = 5 m_{ab}=5 ,  m b ​ c = 3 m_{bc}=3  and  m a ​ c = 2 m_{ac}=2 .
Let  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  be the collection of reflection hyperplanes in  ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3}  arising from the canonical representation  W Γ → G ​ L ​ ( 3 , ℝ ) W_{\Gamma}\to GL(3,\mathbb{R}) . 
 
 
 Let  𝖢 \mathsf{C}  be the simplicial complex obtained by intersecting the unit sphere of  ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3}  with elements in  𝒜 \mathcal{A} . In other words,  𝖢 \mathsf{C}  is the Coxeter complex associated with  W Γ W_{\Gamma} . Each element in  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  gives a  wall  of  𝖢 \mathsf{C} . 
 
 
 Definition 6.1   (Auxiliary sub-arrangement I) . 
 
 We define a sub-collection of walls in  𝖢 \mathsf{C}  as follows. Takes three consecutive vertices  { θ i } i = 1 3 \{\theta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{3}  in a wall of  𝖢 \mathsf{C}  such that  θ 1 \theta_{1}  and  θ 3 \theta_{3}  are of type  c ^ \hat{c} , and  θ 2 \theta_{2}  is type  b ^ \hat{b} . Let  ℋ \mathcal{H}  be the collection of walls of  𝖢 \mathsf{C}  which passes at least one of  θ 1 , θ 2 \theta_{1},\theta_{2}  or  θ 3 \theta_{3} . See Figure  6  left for  ℋ \mathcal{H} . We also think  ℋ \mathcal{H}  as a central arrangement in  ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3} . 
 
 
 Let  H ⊂ ℋ H\subset\mathcal{H}  be a wall passing through  θ 1 \theta_{1} . We consider the deconing of the arrangement  ℋ \mathcal{H}  with respect to  H H . This gives arrangement  ℋ ′ \mathcal{H}^{\prime}  of affine hyperplanes in  ℝ 2 \mathbb{R}^{2} , depicted in Figure  6  right. 
 
 
 
 Figure 6:  Auxiliary sub-arrangement I. 
 
 
 Let  Σ ℋ ′ \Sigma_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}  (resp.  Σ ℋ \Sigma_{\mathcal{H}} ) be the dual polyhedron (cf. Section  2.2 ) associated with  ℋ ′ \mathcal{H}^{\prime}  (resp.  ℋ \mathcal{H} ), see Figure  7  left.
Recall that there is an embedding  i H : Σ ℋ ′ → Σ ℋ i_{H}:\Sigma_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}\to\Sigma_{\mathcal{H}}  whose image is a maximal subcomplex of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  which is contained in one side of  H H . 
 
 
 Let  Σ ^ ℋ ′ \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}  (resp.  Σ ^ ℋ \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{H}} ) be the associated Salvetti complex. Let  X = Σ ℋ ′ X=\Sigma_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}  and  X ^ = Σ ^ ℋ ′ \widehat{X}=\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}} . Then the embedding  i H : Σ ℋ ′ → Σ ℋ i_{H}:\Sigma_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}\to\Sigma_{\mathcal{H}}  induces an embedding  i ^ H : Σ ^ ℋ ′ → Σ ^ ℋ \hat{i}_{H}:\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}\to\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{H}}  which is  π 1 \pi_{1} -injective by Lemma  2.13 . 
 
 
 We now define a collection of subcomplexes of  X X  and  X ^ \widehat{X} .
Denote the four vertical walls of  ℋ ′ \mathcal{H}^{\prime}  by  h 1 , h 2 , h 3 , h 4 h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4}  (from left to right). Let  X i X_{i}  be the union of all closed cell of  X X  which has non-trivial intersection with  h i h_{i} . Let  X ^ i \widehat{X}_{i}  be the subcomplex of  X ^ \widehat{X}  associated with  X i X_{i} . For  i = 1 , 2 , 3 i=1,2,3 , let  Y ^ i = X ^ i ∩ X ^ i + 1 \widehat{Y}_{i}=\widehat{X}_{i}\cap\widehat{X}_{i+1} . 
 
 
 We define a family of subcomplexes  { X i ​ j } 1 ≤ i ≤ 4 , 1 ≤ j ≤ 2 \{X_{ij}\}_{1\leq i\leq 4,1\leq j\leq 2}  of  X X  as follows. For  i = 1 , 3 i=1,3 ,  X i ​ 1 X_{i1}  is the subspace in  X i X_{i}  colored white in Figure  7  (i.e. the hexagonal face), and  X i ​ 2 X_{i2}  is the subspace of  X i X_{i}  colored gray in Figure  7  (i.e. the union of three squares). For  i = 2 , 4 i=2,4 ,  X i ​ 1 X_{i1}  is the subspace in  X i X_{i}  colored black in  X i X_{i}  (i.e. the square on top), and  X i ​ 2 X_{i2}  is the subspace in  X i X_{i}  colored white. Let  X ^ i ​ j \widehat{X}_{ij}  be the subcomplex of  X ^ \widehat{X}  associated with  X i ​ j X_{ij} . 
 
 
 Let  E 1 , E 2 E_{1},E_{2}  be two edges of  X 11 X_{11}  as in Figure  7 , and let  E 3 , E 5 , E 6 E_{3},E_{5},E_{6}  be the edges of  X 13 X_{13}  in Figure  7 . Let  E 4 E_{4}  be the edge in  X 12 X_{12}  in Figure  7 .
For  1 ≤ i ≤ 6 1\leq i\leq 6 , let  E ^ i \widehat{E}_{i}  be the associated subcomplexes of  X ^ \widehat{X} . 
 
 
 Figure 7:  Dual complex. 
 
 
 Lemma 6.2 . 
 
 The group  π 1 ​ X ^ \pi_{1}\widehat{X}  splits as a graph of groups whose underlying graph is a linear graph with 4 vertices. The vertex groups (from left to right) are  π 1 ​ X ^ 1 , π 1 ​ X ^ 2 \pi_{1}\widehat{X}_{1},\pi_{1}\widehat{X}_{2} ,  π 1 ​ X ^ 3 \pi_{1}\widehat{X}_{3}  and  π 1 ​ X ^ 4 \pi_{1}\widehat{X}_{4} ; and the edge groups (from left to right) are  π 1 ​ Y ^ 1 \pi_{1}\widehat{Y}_{1} ,  π 1 ​ Y ^ 2 \pi_{1}\widehat{Y}_{2}  and  π 1 ​ Y ^ 3 \pi_{1}\widehat{Y}_{3} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 It suffices to show the maps  π 1 ​ Y ^ i → π 1 ​ X ^ i \pi_{1}\widehat{Y}_{i}\to\pi_{1}\widehat{X}_{i}  and  π 1 ​ Y ^ i → π 1 ​ X ^ i + 1 \pi_{1}\widehat{Y}_{i}\to\pi_{1}\widehat{X}_{i+1}  induced by inclusion are injective for  i = 1 , 2 , 3 i=1,2,3 . We only show this for  π 1 ​ Y ^ 1 → π 1 ​ X ^ 1 \pi_{1}\widehat{Y}_{1}\to\pi_{1}\widehat{X}_{1} , as the other inclusions are similar. Note that  π 1 ​ X ^ 1 \pi_{1}\widehat{X}_{1}  splits as amalgamation 
 

 
 
 π 1 ​ X ^ 11 ∗ π 1 ​ A π 1 ​ X ^ 12 \pi_{1}\widehat{X}_{11}*_{\pi_{1}A}\pi_{1}\widehat{X}_{12} 
 
 
 where  A = X ^ 11 ∩ X ^ 12 A=\widehat{X}_{11}\cap\widehat{X}_{12} , as  π 1 ​ A → π 1 ​ X ^ 11 \pi_{1}A\to\pi_{1}\widehat{X}_{11}  is injective (because of the retraction  X ^ 11 → A \widehat{X}_{11}\to A  as in Definition  2.9 ) and  π 1 ​ A → π 1 ​ X ^ 12 \pi_{1}A\to\pi_{1}\widehat{X}_{12}  is injective (because  X ^ 12 \widehat{X}_{12}  is a product of  A A  and  X ^ 12 ∩ Y ^ 1 \widehat{X}_{12}\cap\widehat{Y}_{1} ).
By  [ Ser02 , Page 6, Proposition 3] , the injectivity of  π 1 ​ Y ^ 1 → π 1 ​ X ^ 1 \pi_{1}\widehat{Y}_{1}\to\pi_{1}\widehat{X}_{1}  would follow if we can show  π 1 ​ A ∩ π 1 ​ ( X ^ 11 ∩ Y ^ 1 ) \pi_{1}A\cap\pi_{1}(\widehat{X}_{11}\cap\widehat{Y}_{1})  is trivial in  π 1 ​ X ^ 11 \pi_{1}\widehat{X}_{11} , and  π 1 ​ A ∩ π 1 ​ ( X ^ 12 ∩ Y ^ 1 ) \pi_{1}A\cap\pi_{1}(\widehat{X}_{12}\cap\widehat{Y}_{1})  is trivial in  π 1 ​ X ^ 12 \pi_{1}\widehat{X}_{12} . The first statement follows by considering the retraction  X ^ 11 → A \widehat{X}_{11}\to A  which maps  X ^ 12 ∩ Y ^ 1 \widehat{X}_{12}\cap\widehat{Y}_{1}  to a single point, and the second statement follows from the product structure of  X ^ 12 \widehat{X}_{12} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma 6.3 . 
 
 Let  K ~ \widetilde{K}  be the universal cover of  X ^ 1 ∪ X ^ 2 \widehat{X}_{1}\cup\widehat{X}_{2} . Let  T 1 T_{1}  be a lift of  X ^ i ​ j \widehat{X}_{ij}  in  K ~ \widetilde{K} , and let  T 2 T_{2}  be a lift  X ^ i ′ ​ j ′ \widehat{X}_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}} , with  1 ≤ i , i ′ , j , j ′ ≤ 2 1\leq i,i^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}\leq 2 . If  T 1 ∩ T 2 ≠ ∅ T_{1}\cap T_{2}\neq\emptyset , then  T 1 ∩ T 2 T_{1}\cap T_{2}  is connected. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We assume  X ^ i ​ j = X ^ 11 \widehat{X}_{ij}=\widehat{X}_{11}  and  X ^ i ′ ​ j ′ = X ^ 22 \widehat{X}_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}=\widehat{X}_{22} . The other cases are similar. Suppose  T 1 ∩ T 2 T_{1}\cap T_{2}  is not connected. Then we take two connected components  S 1 S_{1}  and  S 2 S_{2}  of  T 1 ∩ T 2 T_{1}\cap T_{2} . Then  S 1 S_{1}  and  S 2 S_{2}  are two different lifts of  X ^ 11 ∩ X ^ 22 \widehat{X}_{11}\cap\widehat{X}_{22} . For  i = 1 , 2 i=1,2 , let  P ~ i ⊂ T i \widetilde{P}_{i}\subset T_{i}  be a path from  x 1 ∈ S 1 x_{1}\in S_{1}  to  x 2 ∈ S 2 x_{2}\in S_{2} . Let  P i ⊂ X ^ 1 ∪ X ^ 2 P_{i}\subset\widehat{X}_{1}\cup\widehat{X}_{2}  be the image of  P ~ i \widetilde{P}_{i}  under the covering map. Then  P 1 P_{1}  and  P 2 P_{2}  are homotopic rel endpoints in  X ^ 1 ∪ X ^ 2 \widehat{X}_{1}\cup\widehat{X}_{2} . Now consider the retraction map  r : X ^ → X ^ 11 r:\widehat{X}\to\widehat{X}_{11}  (cf. Definition  2.9 ) which restricts to a retraction map  r : X ^ 1 ∪ X ^ 2 → X ^ 11 r:\widehat{X}_{1}\cup\widehat{X}_{2}\to\widehat{X}_{11} . Then  r ⁡ ( P 1 ) = P 1 r(P_{1})=P_{1}  and  r ⁡ ( P 2 ) r(P_{2})  are homotopic rel endpoints in  X ^ 11 \widehat{X}_{11} . As  r ⁡ ( X ^ 22 ) = X ^ 11 ∩ X ^ 22 r(\widehat{X}_{22})=\widehat{X}_{11}\cap\widehat{X}_{22} , we know  r ⁡ ( P 2 ) ⊂ X ^ 11 ∩ X ^ 22 r(P_{2})\subset\widehat{X}_{11}\cap\widehat{X}_{22} . Thus  P 1 P_{1}  is homotopic rel endpoints in  X ^ 11 \widehat{X}_{11}  to a path in  X ^ 11 ∩ X ^ 22 \widehat{X}_{11}\cap\widehat{X}_{22} . Hence  S 1 = S 2 S_{1}=S_{2} , which is a contradiction, and the lemma is proved.
∎ 
 
 
 
 We now define a simplex complex of group structure  𝒰 \mathcal{U}  on  π 1 ​ ( X ^ 1 ∪ X ^ 2 ) \pi_{1}(\widehat{X}_{1}\cup\widehat{X}_{2})  as in Figure  6.1 , where the underlying complex  U U  is a union of two triangles and all the groups over 2-faces are trivial. Local groups over vertices and edges are fundamental groups of subcomplexes of  X ^ \widehat{X}  as labeled in Figure  6.1 . The morphisms between the local groups are induced by inclusions of the associated subcomplexes. By Lemma  2.13 , all the morphisms between the local groups are injective, hence  𝒰 \mathcal{U}  is a simple complex of groups. Moreover, it follows from Lemma  6.2  that each local group injects into  π 1 ​ ( X ^ 1 ∪ X ^ 2 ) \pi_{1}(\widehat{X}_{1}\cup\widehat{X}_{2}) . Thus  𝒰 \mathcal{U}  is developable with  π 1 ​ ( X ^ 1 ∪ X ^ 2 ) = π 1 ​ 𝒰 \pi_{1}(\widehat{X}_{1}\cup\widehat{X}_{2})=\pi_{1}\mathcal{U} . 
 
 
 
 
 Let  𝕌 \mathbb{U}  be the development complex of  𝒰 \mathcal{U}  (cf.  [ BH99 , Chapter II.12] ). We now give an alternative description of  𝕌 \mathbb{U} . Let  K ~ \widetilde{K}  be the universal cover of  X ^ 1 ∪ X ^ 2 \widehat{X}_{1}\cup\widehat{X}_{2} . Vertices of  𝕌 \mathbb{U}  are in 1-1 correspondence with lifts of  X ^ i ​ j \widehat{X}_{ij}  in  K ~ \widetilde{K}  ( 1 ≤ i ≤ 2 , 1 ≤ j ≤ 2 1\leq i\leq 2,1\leq j\leq 2 ). These lifts are called  standard subcomplexes  of  K ~ \widetilde{K} .
Each vertex of  𝕌 \mathbb{U}  has a well-defined  type , which is one of  { X ^ i ​ j } 1 ≤ i ≤ 2 , 1 ≤ j ≤ 2 \{\widehat{X}_{ij}\}_{1\leq i\leq 2,1\leq j\leq 2} . Two vertices of  𝕌 \mathbb{U}  are adjacent in  𝕌 \mathbb{U}  if their associated subcomplexes of  K ~ \widetilde{K}  have non-empty intersection, and their types correspond to adjacent vertices in  U U . Three mutually adjacent vertices of  𝕌 \mathbb{U}  form a triangle, if their associated subcomplexes have non-empty common intersection. From this description, we also see that  𝕌 \mathbb{U}  is simply connected, as  K ~ \widetilde{K}  is simply connected. 
 
 
 There is a natural action  π 1 ​ ( X ^ 1 ∪ X ^ 2 ) ↷ 𝕌 \pi_{1}(\widehat{X}_{1}\cup\widehat{X}_{2})\curvearrowright\mathbb{U} , with the quotient complex being  U U . We metrize  U U  such that it is made of two flat right-angled isosceles triangles, with right-angles at the vertices labeled by  π 1 ​ ( X ^ 21 ) \pi_{1}(\widehat{X}_{21})  and  π 1 ​ ( X ^ 12 ) \pi_{1}(\widehat{X}_{12}) . This pulls back to a piecewise Euclidean metric on  𝕌 \mathbb{U} . 
 
 
 Lemma 6.4 . 
 
 𝕌 \mathbb{U}  is CAT ( 0 ) (0) . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 As  𝕌 \mathbb{U}  is simply-connected, we need to the link of each vertex is CAT ( 1 ) (1) . As  X ^ 12 \widehat{X}_{12}  splits as a product of  X ^ 11 ∩ X ^ 12 \widehat{X}_{11}\cap\widehat{X}_{12}  and  X ^ 12 ∩ X ^ 22 \widehat{X}_{12}\cap\widehat{X}_{22} , we know the link of a vertex of type  X ^ 22 \widehat{X}_{22}  is a complete bipartite graph with each edge having length  = π / 2 =\pi/2 . Thus this link is CAT ( 1 ) (1) . Similarly the link at any vertex of type  X ^ 21 \widehat{X}_{21}  is CAT ( 1 ) (1) . 
 
 
 Now we look at a vertex  v ∈ 𝕌 v\in\mathbb{U}  of type  X ^ 22 \widehat{X}_{22} . Let  Γ \Gamma  be its link.
Let 
 D 1 = X ^ 22 ∩ X ^ 21 D_{1}=\widehat{X}_{22}\cap\widehat{X}_{21} ,  D 2 = X ^ 22 ∩ X ^ 11 D_{2}=\widehat{X}_{22}\cap\widehat{X}_{11}  and  D 3 = X ^ 22 ∩ X ^ 12 D_{3}=\widehat{X}_{22}\cap\widehat{X}_{12} . 
 Then vertices of  Γ \Gamma  are in one to one correspondence with lifts of  D i D_{i}  in  X ~ 22 \widetilde{X}_{22}  for  1 ≤ i ≤ 3 1\leq i\leq 3 . Two vertices are adjacent if and only if the associated lifts have non-trivial intersection. We say a vertex of  Γ \Gamma  is of type  D i D_{i}  if it corresponds to a lift of  D i D_{i} . Each edge of  Γ \Gamma  has length  = π / 4 =\pi/4 . 
 
 
 Take an embedded cycle in  Γ \Gamma  with consecutive vertices  { w i } i ∈ ℤ / n ​ ℤ \{w_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}} . Let  X ~ i \widetilde{X}_{i}  be the subcomplex corresponding to  w i w_{i} . Let  P ~ i \widetilde{P}_{i}  be an edge path from  X ~ i − 1 ∩ X ~ i \widetilde{X}_{i-1}\cap\widetilde{X}_{i}  to  X ~ i ∩ X ~ i + 1 \widetilde{X}_{i}\cap\widetilde{X}_{i+1} . Let  P ~ \widetilde{P}  be the loop obtained by the concatenation of  { P ~ i } i = 1 n \{\widetilde{P}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n} , which projects to a loop  P = P 1 P 2 ⋯ P n P=P_{1}P_{2}\cdots P_{n}  in  X ^ 22 \widehat{X}_{22}  which is null-homotopic in  X ^ 22 \widehat{X}_{22} . We can assume  P P  does not backtrack locally. Given a hyperplane  H ∈ ℋ ′ H\in\mathcal{H}^{\prime}  as in Definition  6.7 , an  H H -segment of  P P  is a maximal subpath of  Q ⊂ P Q\subset P  such that the image of  Q Q  under the projection to the dual polyhedron  X ^ → X \widehat{X}\to X  is a single edge dual to  H H . By  [ Fal95 , Lemma 3.6] , for each hyperplane  H H  of  ℋ ′ \mathcal{H}^{\prime}  dual to  X 22 X_{22} ,  P P  contains at least two disjoint  H H -segments. 
 
 
 It follows that  { w i } i ∈ ℤ / n ​ ℤ \{w_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}}  contains at least two different members of type  D 1 D_{1} , two different members of type  D 2 D_{2}  and one member of type  D 3 D_{3} . As each edge of  Γ \Gamma  has length  π / 4 \pi/4  and  Γ \Gamma  is bipartite, the only possibility of such cycle having length  < 2 ​ π <2\pi  is the case of the cycle has 6 vertices, with  w 1 , w 5 w_{1},w_{5}  being of type  D 1 D_{1} ,  w 2 , w 4 , w 6 w_{2},w_{4},w_{6}  being of type  D 2 D_{2}  and  w 3 w_{3}  being of type  D 3 D_{3} . In this case  P 1 , P 5 P_{1},P_{5}  are loops based at  D 1 ∩ D 2 D_{1}\cap D_{2} ,  P 3 P_{3}  is a loop based at  D 2 ∩ D 3 D_{2}\cap D_{3}  and  P 6 P_{6}  is a loop based at  D 1 ∩ D 2 D_{1}\cap D_{2} . 
 
 
 By consider the image of  P P  under the retraction (cf. Definition  2.9 )  Π D 1 : X ^ 22 → D 1 \Pi_{D_{1}}:\widehat{X}_{22}\to D_{1} , we know the concatenation  P 1 ​ P 5 P_{1}P_{5}  is null-homotopic in  D 1 D_{1} . Thus we can assume  P 5 = P 1 − 1 P_{5}=P^{-1}_{1}  (i.e.  P 5 P_{5}  is the inverse path of  P 1 P_{1} ). As  P P  is null-homotopic in  X ^ 22 \widehat{X}_{22} , we know  [ P 2 ​ P 3 ​ P 4 ] = [ P 1 − 1 ​ P 6 − 1 ​ P 1 ] [P_{2}P_{3}P_{4}]=[P^{-1}_{1}P^{-1}_{6}P_{1}]  represents the same element in  π 1 ​ ( X ^ 22 , x 0 ) \pi_{1}(\widehat{X}_{22},x_{0})  where  x 0 = D 1 ∩ D 2 x_{0}=D_{1}\cap D_{2}  (we use  [ ⋯ ] [\cdots]  to denotes homotopy class of paths in  X ^ 22 \widehat{X}_{22}  rel end points). By Lemma  2.13 ,  [ P 1 ] = [ Q ​ R ] [P_{1}]=[QR]  where  Q , R Q,R  are loops,  Q ⊂ D 2 ∪ D 3 Q\subset D_{2}\cup D_{3}  and  [ R ] [R]  is in the center of  π 1 ​ ( X ^ 22 , x 0 ) \pi_{1}(\widehat{X}_{22},x_{0}) . Thus  [ P 1 − 1 ​ P 6 − 1 ​ P 1 ] = [ Q − 1 ​ P 6 − 1 ​ Q ] [P^{-1}_{1}P^{-1}_{6}P_{1}]=[Q^{-1}P^{-1}_{6}Q] . 
 
 
 We decompose  Q Q  into subsegments alternating between  D 2 D_{2}  and  D 3 D_{3}  as  Q = Q 1 Q 2 ⋯ Q n Q=Q_{1}Q_{2}\cdots Q_{n}  such that each  Q i Q_{i}  is a maximal sub-segment of  Q Q  in  D 2 D_{2}  or  D 3 D_{3} . We can assume each  Q i Q_{i}  is either a homotopically non-trivial loop in the associated  D 2 D_{2}  or  D 3 D_{3} , or a path with two different endpoints. As  Q Q  is a loop, we know that  n n  is always an odd number. Moreover  n ≥ 3 n\geq 3  as  Q Q  is not homotopic rel endpoints in  X ^ 22 \widehat{X}_{22}  to a loop contained in  D 2 D_{2}  - otherwise  P 1 P_{1}  and  P 6 P_{6}  give commuting elements in  π 1 ​ ( X ^ 22 ) \pi_{1}(\widehat{X}_{22}) , which is a contradiction as Lemma  2.13  implies that  P 1 P_{1}  and  P 6 P_{6}  generate a free group in  π 1 ​ ( X ^ 22 ) \pi_{1}(\widehat{X}_{22}) . 
 
 
 Now we can write 
 

 
 
 [ Q n − 1 Q n − 1 − 1 ⋯ Q 1 − 1 P 6 − 1 Q 1 ⋯ Q n ] = [ P 2 P 3 P 4 ] . [Q^{-1}_{n}Q^{-1}_{n-1}\cdots Q^{-1}_{1}P^{-1}_{6}Q_{1}\cdots Q_{n}]=[P_{2}P_{3}P_{4}]. 
 
 
 By Lemma  2.13 , 
 Q − 1 n Q − 1 n − 1 ⋯ Q − 1 1 P − 1 6 Q − 1 1 ⋯ Q n Q^{-1}_{n}Q^{-1}_{n-1}\cdots Q^{-1}_{1}P^{-1}_{6}Q^{-1}_{1}\cdots Q_{n}  and  P 2 ​ P 3 ​ P 4 P_{2}P_{3}P_{4} 
 represent the same element in  π 1 ​ ( D 2 ∪ D 3 ) \pi_{1}(D_{2}\cup D_{3}) . As  n ≥ 3 n\geq 3 , we know 
 

 
 
 Q − 1 n Q − 1 n − 1 ⋯ Q − 1 1 P − 1 6 Q 1 ⋯ Q n Q^{-1}_{n}Q^{-1}_{n-1}\cdots Q^{-1}_{1}P^{-1}_{6}Q_{1}\cdots Q_{n} 
 
 
 is a concatenation of at least 5 sub-segments alternating in  D 2 D_{2}  and  D 3 D_{3}  such that each of them is either a homotopically non-trivial loop in the associated  D 2 D_{2}  or  D 3 D_{3}  (note that  Q 1 − 1 ​ P 6 − 1 ​ Q 1 Q^{-1}_{1}P^{-1}_{6}Q_{1}  is homotopically non-trivial in  D 2 D_{2}  as  P 6 − 1 P^{-1}_{6}  is), or a path with two different endpoints. On the other hand,  P 2 ​ P 3 ​ P 4 P_{2}P_{3}P_{4}  only has 3 such sub-segments. 
 
 
 We view  D 2 ∪ D 3 D_{2}\cup D_{3}  as a graph of spaces, with the underlying graph being a single edge. The two vertex spaces are  D 2 D_{2}  and  D 3 D_{3} , and the edge space is a single point  D 2 ∩ D 3 D_{2}\cap D_{3} . Let  T T  be the associated Bass-Serre tree. Then  Q − 1 n Q − 1 n − 1 ⋯ Q 1 P − 1 6 Q 1 ⋯ Q n Q^{-1}_{n}Q^{-1}_{n-1}\cdots Q_{1}P^{-1}_{6}Q_{1}\cdots Q_{n}  gives a geodesic segment in  T T  with at least 5 vertices, while  P 2 ​ P 3 ​ P 4 P_{2}P_{3}P_{4}  gives a geodesic segment with 3 vertices. Thus we can not have  [ Q n − 1 Q n − 1 − 1 ⋯ Q 1 P 6 − 1 Q 1 ⋯ Q n ] = [ P 2 P 3 P 4 ] [Q^{-1}_{n}Q^{-1}_{n-1}\cdots Q_{1}P^{-1}_{6}Q_{1}\cdots Q_{n}]=[P_{2}P_{3}P_{4}] . It follows that  Γ \Gamma  is CAT ( 1 ) (1) . 
 
 
 It remains to verify the link at a vertex of type  X ^ 11 \widehat{X}_{11} . Each edge in the link again has length  = π / 4 =\pi/4 . The girth of the link is  ≥ 8 \geq 8  by  [ Fal95 , Lemma 3.6]  and the argument as before.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Let  Z ^ = X ^ 11 ∪ X ^ 22 ∪ X ^ 21 \widehat{Z}=\widehat{X}_{11}\cup\widehat{X}_{22}\cup\widehat{X}_{21} . Then there is an embedding  Z ^ → Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{Z}\to\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  defined as follows. Consider  Z ^ ⊂ X ^ = Σ ^ ℋ ′ → Σ ^ ℋ \widehat{Z}\subset\widehat{X}=\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}\to\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{H}}  where the second map is the map  i ^ H \hat{i}_{H}  after Definition  6.1 . Let  c : Σ 𝒜 → Σ ℋ c:\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}\to\Sigma_{\mathcal{H}}  and  c ^ : Σ ^ 𝒜 → Σ ^ ℋ \hat{c}:\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}\to\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{H}}  be as in Section  2.5 . Note that there is a unique face  F F  of  Σ 𝒜 \Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}  such that  c ⁡ ( F ) = i H ​ ( X 11 ) c(F)=i_{H}(X_{11}) . Moreover, the collection of elements of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  dual to  F F  is identical to the collection of elements of  ℋ \mathcal{H}  dual to  i H ​ ( X 11 ) i_{H}(X_{11}) . Thus  c c  maps  F F  homeomorphically to  i H ​ ( X 11 ) i_{H}(X_{11}) , and consequently  c ^ \hat{c}  map  F ^ \widehat{F}  homeomorphically to  i ^ H ​ ( X ^ 11 ) \hat{i}_{H}(\widehat{X}_{11}) . Similarly discussion applies to  i ^ H ​ ( X ^ 22 ) \hat{i}_{H}(\widehat{X}_{22})  and  i ^ H ​ ( X ^ 21 ) \hat{i}_{H}(\widehat{X}_{21}) , which gives a lift  i ^ H ​ ( Z ^ ) \hat{i}_{H}(\widehat{Z})  to a subcomplex of  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}} . Let  i : Z ^ → Σ ^ 𝒜 i:\widehat{Z}\to\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}  be such embedding. 
 
 
 Lemma 6.5 . 
 
 Let  P ⊂ Z ^ P\subset\widehat{Z}  be a loop. If  i ⁡ ( P ) i(P)  is null-homotopic in  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}} , then  P P  is null-homotopic in  X ^ 1 ∪ X ^ 2 \widehat{X}_{1}\cup\widehat{X}_{2} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Note that if  i ⁡ ( P ) i(P)  is null-homotopic in  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}} , then  c ^ ∘ i ⁡ ( P ) = i ^ H ​ ( P ) \hat{c}\circ i(P)=\hat{i}_{H}(P)  is null-homotopic in  Σ ^ ℋ \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{H}} . Thus  P P  is null-homotopic in  X ^ = Σ ^ ℋ ′ \widehat{X}=\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}  by Lemma  2.13 . Now Lemma  6.2  implies  P P  is null-homotopic in  X ^ 1 ∪ X ^ 2 \widehat{X}_{1}\cup\widehat{X}_{2} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 We end this section by recording the following lemma for later use. For three points  x , y , z x,y,z  in a CAT ( 0 ) (0)  space, we use  ∠ y ​ ( x , z ) \angle_{y}(x,z)  to denote the Alexandrov between the geodesic segments  y ​ x ¯ \overline{yx}  and  y ​ z ¯ \overline{yz}  (cf.  [ BH99 , Chapter II.3.1] ). 
 
 
 Lemma 6.6 . 
 
 Let  K ~ \widetilde{K}  be the universal cover of  X ^ 1 ∪ X ^ 2 \widehat{X}_{1}\cup\widehat{X}_{2} .
Let  { z i } i = 1 3 \{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{3}  be three consecutive vertices in  𝕌 \mathbb{U} . Let  { T i } i = 1 3 \{T_{i}\}_{i=1}^{3}  be the associated standard subcomplexes in  K ~ \widetilde{K} . We assume one of the following situations holds: 
 
 1.  
 
 z 1 , z 2 , z 3 z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}  are of type  X ^ 12 , X ^ 11 , X ^ 12 \widehat{X}_{12},\widehat{X}_{11},\widehat{X}_{12}  respectively and there is a path from  T 1 ∩ T 2 T_{1}\cap T_{2}  to  T 3 ∩ T 2 T_{3}\cap T_{2}  which projects to a homotopically nontrivial loop in  X ^ 11 ∩ X ^ 21 \widehat{X}_{11}\cap\widehat{X}_{21}  or  E ^ 1 \widehat{E}_{1} ; 
 
 2.  
 
 z 1 , z 2 , z 3 z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}  are of type  X ^ 21 , X ^ 22 , X ^ 21 \widehat{X}_{21},\widehat{X}_{22},\widehat{X}_{21}  respectively and there is a path from  T 1 ∩ T 2 T_{1}\cap T_{2}  to  T 3 ∩ T 2 T_{3}\cap T_{2}  which projects to a homotopically nontrivial loop in  E ^ 6 \hat{E}_{6} ; 
 
 3.  
 
 z 1 , z 2 , z 3 z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}  are of type  X ^ 22 , X ^ 11 , X ^ 22 \widehat{X}_{22},\widehat{X}_{11},\widehat{X}_{22}  or type  X ^ 21 , X ^ 11 , X ^ 21 \widehat{X}_{21},\widehat{X}_{11},\widehat{X}_{21}  respectively and there is a path from  T 1 ∩ T 2 T_{1}\cap T_{2}  to  T 3 ∩ T 2 T_{3}\cap T_{2}  which projects to a homotopically nontrivial loop in  E ^ 2 \widehat{E}_{2} . 
 
 
 Then  ∠ z 2 ​ ( z 1 , z 3 ) = π \angle_{z_{2}}(z_{1},z_{3})=\pi . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  z ¯ i \bar{z}_{i}  be the projection of  z i z_{i}  under the map  p : 𝕌 → U p:\mathbb{U}\to U  induced by the action of  π 1 ​ ( X ^ 1 ∪ X ^ 2 ) \pi_{1}(\widehat{X}_{1}\cup\widehat{X}_{2}) . We view  z 1 z_{1}  and  z 3 z_{3}  as vertices in the link  lk ⁡ ( z 2 , 𝕌 ) \operatorname{lk}(z_{2},\mathbb{U})  of  z z  in  𝕌 \mathbb{U} . Then any path in  lk ⁡ ( z 2 , 𝕌 ) \operatorname{lk}(z_{2},\mathbb{U})  joining  z 1 z_{1}  and  z 3 z_{3}  projects to path in  lk ⁡ ( z ¯ 2 , U ) \operatorname{lk}(\bar{z}_{2},U)  joining  z ¯ 1 \bar{z}_{1}  and  z ¯ 3 \bar{z}_{3} .
For (1), note that  z ¯ 1 = z ¯ 3 \bar{z}_{1}=\bar{z}_{3} , thus  ∠ z 2 ​ ( z 1 , z 3 ) \angle_{z_{2}}(z_{1},z_{3})  is a multiple of  π / 2 \pi/2 . Now look at the  E ^ 1 \widehat{E}_{1}  case of (1). If  ∠ z 2 ​ ( z 1 , z 3 ) = π / 2 \angle_{z_{2}}(z_{1},z_{3})=\pi/2 , then there is a vertex  z ∈ 𝕌 z\in\mathbb{U}  such that it is adjacent to  z 1 , z 2 , z 3 z_{1},z_{2},z_{3} . Let  T T  be the subcomplex of  K ~ \widetilde{K}  associated with  z z . Note that  z z  has type  X ^ 22 \widehat{X}_{22} . As  z 1 , z 2 , z z_{1},z_{2},z  span a triangle, then  T 1 ∩ T 2 ∩ T ≠ ∅ T_{1}\cap T_{2}\cap T\neq\emptyset . Hence this triple intersection is a vertex, which we denoted by  x 1 x_{1} . Similarly, let  x 2 = T 2 ∩ T ∩ T 3 x_{2}=T_{2}\cap T\cap T_{3} . 
 
 
 Let  P ~ \widetilde{P}  be the path from a point in  y 1 ∈ T 1 ∩ T 2 y_{1}\in T_{1}\cap T_{2}  to a point in  y 2 ∈ T 2 ∩ T 3 y_{2}\in T_{2}\cap T_{3}  as in (1). Let  P ~ ′ \widetilde{P}^{\prime}  be the concatenation of a path  P ~ 1 ′ ⊂ T 1 ∩ T 2 \widetilde{P}^{\prime}_{1}\subset T_{1}\cap T_{2}  from  y 1 y_{1}  to  x 1 x_{1} , a path  P ~ 2 ′ ⊂ T 2 ∩ T \widetilde{P}^{\prime}_{2}\subset T_{2}\cap T  from  x 1 x_{1}  to  x 2 x_{2}  and a path  P ~ 3 ′ ⊂ T 2 ∩ T 3 \widetilde{P}^{\prime}_{3}\subset T_{2}\cap T_{3}  from  x 2 x_{2}  to  y 2 y_{2} . These paths exist by Lemma  6.3 . Let  P P  and  P ′ P^{\prime}  be the image of  P ~ \widetilde{P}  and  P ~ ′ \widetilde{P}^{\prime}  in  X ^ 1 ∪ X ^ 2 \widehat{X}_{1}\cup\widehat{X}_{2}  under the covering map. Then  P ⊂ E ^ 1 P\subset\widehat{E}_{1}  and  P ′ ⊂ ( X ^ 11 ∩ X ^ 12 ) ∪ ( X ^ 11 ∩ X ^ 22 ) P^{\prime}\subset(\widehat{X}_{11}\cap\widehat{X}_{12})\cup(\widehat{X}_{11}\cap\widehat{X}_{22}) . Now we consider the retraction  r : X ^ → E ^ r:\widehat{X}\to\widehat{E}  as in Definition  2.9  which restricts to  r : X ^ 11 → E ^ 1 r:\widehat{X}_{11}\to\widehat{E}_{1} . As  P P  and  P ′ P^{\prime}  are homotopic rel endpoints in  X ^ 1 ∪ X ^ 2 \widehat{X}_{1}\cup\widehat{X}_{2} , we know  r ⁡ ( P ) = P r(P)=P  and  r ⁡ ( P ′ ) r(P^{\prime})  are homotopic rel endpoints in  E ^ 1 \widehat{E}_{1} . However, this is a contradiction as  r ⁡ ( P ′ ) r(P^{\prime})  is a point. This finishes the proof of the  E ^ 1 \widehat{E}_{1}  case of (1).
The  X ^ 11 ∩ X ^ 21 \widehat{X}_{11}\cap\widehat{X}_{21}  case of (1), as well as (2) and (3) follows from a similar argument.
∎

 Let  𝖢 \mathsf{C}  and  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  be as before, defined from the Coxeter group of type  H 3 H_{3} . 
 
 
 Definition 6.7   (Auxiliary sub-arrangement II) . 
 
 We define a sub-collection of walls in  𝖢 \mathsf{C}  as follows. Takes four consecutive vertices  { θ i } i = 1 4 \{\theta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{4}  in a wall of  𝖢 \mathsf{C}  such that  θ 1 \theta_{1}  is of type  c ^ \hat{c} ,  θ 2 , θ 4 \theta_{2},\theta_{4}  are of type  a ^ \hat{a}  and  θ 3 \theta_{3}  is of type  b ^ \hat{b} . We also think  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  as a central arrangement in  ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3} . 
 
 
 Let  H ⊂ 𝒦 H\subset\mathcal{K}  be the wall passing through  θ 1 \theta_{1}  as in the boundary circle of Figure  8  left. We consider the deconing of the arrangement  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  with respect to  H H . This gives us a planar arrangement  𝒦 ′ \mathcal{K}^{\prime} , depicted in Figure  8  right.

 We refer to Figure  9 . Denote the four vertical walls of  𝒦 ′ \mathcal{K}^{\prime}  by  h 1 , h 2 , h 3 , h 4 h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4}  (from left to right).  Lemma 6.8 . 
 
 The map  𝖷 ^ ′ \widehat{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}  to  𝖷 ^ \widehat{\mathsf{X}}  is  π 1 \pi_{1} -injective. 
 
 
 
 This follows from the analogue of Lemma  6.2  in the setting of  π 1 ​ 𝖷 ^ \pi_{1}\widehat{\mathsf{X}} , which can be proved by a similar argument as Lemma  6.2 . 
 
 
 Lemma 6.9 . 
 
 Let  ω \omega  be a loop in  𝖸 ^ \widehat{\mathsf{Y}} . If  i ⁡ ( ω ) i(\omega)  is null-homotopic in  Σ ^ 𝒜 \widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{A}} , then  ω \omega  is null-homotopic in  𝖷 ^ ′ \widehat{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime} . 
 
 
 
 The proof of this lemma is almost identical to Lemma  6.5 , using Lemma  6.8 .

 7  One side flagness in  H 3 H_{3} 

 
 The main goal of this section is to prove the following. 
 
 
 Theorem 7.1 . 
 
 Let  Λ \Lambda  be the Dynkin diagram of type  H 3 H_{3}  with vertex set  { a , b , c } \{a,b,c\}  such that  m a ​ b = 5 m_{ab}=5 ,  m b ​ c = 3 m_{bc}=3 .
Let  Δ \Delta  be the Artin complex of the Artin group  A Λ A_{\Lambda}  with the order on its vertex induced from  a < b < c a<b<c  (see Definition  3.12 ). Then  Δ \Delta  is downward flag. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 For  i ∈ ℤ / 3 ​ ℤ i\in\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z} , let  x i x_{i}  be a lower bound of  y i y_{i}  and  y i + 1 y_{i+1} . We assume  { x 1 , x 2 , x 3 } \{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\}  are pairwise distinct, otherwise the theorem is clear.
If one of  { y 1 , y 2 , y 3 } \{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\} , say  y 1 y_{1} , is of type  a ^ \hat{a} , then we must have  y 1 ≤ y 2 y_{1}\leq y_{2}  and  y 1 ≤ y 3 y_{1}\leq y_{3} , and the theorem is clear. So we assume none of  { y 1 , y 2 , y 3 } \{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\}  is of type  a ^ \hat{a} . 
 
 
 If each of  { y 1 , y 2 , y 3 } \{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\}  is of type  c ^ \hat{c} , then we can each pair of them has a lower bound which is a type  a ^ \hat{a}  vertex (if a pair has a type  b ^ \hat{b}  vertex as lower bound, then we can always find a type  a ^ \hat{a}  vertex which is  ≤ \leq  any given type  b ^ \hat{b}  vertex). Then the theorem follows immediately from Lemma  7.2  below. 
 
 
 If exactly two of  { y 1 , y 2 , y 3 } \{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\}  is of type  c ^ \hat{c} . We assume without loss of generality that  y 1 y_{1}  is of type  b ^ \hat{b} . As before, we assume each  x i x_{i}  is of type  a ^ \hat{a} .
Let  y 1 ′ y^{\prime}_{1}  be a vertex of type  c ^ \hat{c}  such that  y 1 ≤ y 1 ′ y_{1}\leq y^{\prime}_{1} . Lemma  7.2  gives a vertex  s s  which is a common lower bound of  { y 1 ′ , y 2 , y 3 } \{y^{\prime}_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\} . We can assume  s s  is of type  a ^ \hat{a} . If  s s  is one of  x 1 , x 3 x_{1},x_{3} , then  s s  is common lower bound of  { y 1 , y 2 , y 3 } \{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\} . It remains to consider the case  s ≠ x 1 s\neq x_{1}  and  s ≠ x 3 s\neq x_{3} . Note that  y 1 ′ , s , y 3 , x 3 y^{\prime}_{1},s,y_{3},x_{3}  form a 4-cycle in  Σ ^ \widehat{\Sigma}  made of type  a ^ \hat{a}  and type  c ^ \hat{c}  vertices. By Theorem  3.17 , there exists a type  b ^ \hat{b}  vertex  z 3 z_{3}  such that  z 3 z_{3}  is adjacent to each of  { y 1 ′ , s , y 3 , x 3 } \{y^{\prime}_{1},s,y_{3},x_{3}\} . Similarly, there exists a type  b ^ \hat{b}  vertex  z 1 z_{1}  such that  z 1 z_{1}  is adjacent to each of  { x 1 , y 2 , s , y 1 ′ } \{x_{1},y_{2},s,y^{\prime}_{1}\} . Now we consider the 6-cycle 
 

 
 
 x 3 → z 3 → s → z 1 → x 1 → y 1 → x 3 . x_{3}\to z_{3}\to s\to z_{1}\to x_{1}\to y_{1}\to x_{3}. 
 
 
 Each vertex in this 6-cycle is adjacent to  y 1 ′ y^{\prime}_{1} . However, as  y 1 ′ y^{\prime}_{1}  is of type  c ^ \hat{c} , we know from  [ Cri05 , Lemma 39]  that the girth of Lk ( y 1 ′ , Δ ) (y^{\prime}_{1},\Delta)  is  10 10 . Thus the image of this 6-cycle in Lk ( y 1 ′ , Δ ) (y^{\prime}_{1},\Delta)  is a tree. On the other hand, as we assume  { x 1 , s , x 3 } \{x_{1},s,x_{3}\}  is pairwise distinct, the only possibility  z 1 = z 3 = y 1 z_{1}=z_{3}=y_{1} . Thus  s s  is adjacent to  y 1 y_{1} , hence  s ≤ y 1 s\leq y_{1}  and  s s  is a lower bound for  { y 1 , y 2 , y 3 } \{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\} . 
 
 
 If exactly one of  { y 1 , y 2 , y 3 } \{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\}  is of type  c ^ \hat{c} . We assume without loss of generality that  y 1 y_{1}  and  y 2 y_{2}  are of type  b ^ \hat{b} . As before, we assume each  x i x_{i}  is of type  a ^ \hat{a} .
For  i = 1 , 2 i=1,2 , let  y i ′ y^{\prime}_{i}  be a vertex of type  c ^ \hat{c}  such that  y i ≤ y i ′ y_{i}\leq y^{\prime}_{i} . Lemma  7.2  gives a vertex  s s  which is a common lower bound of  { y 1 ′ , y 2 ′ , y 3 } \{y^{\prime}_{1},y^{\prime}_{2},y_{3}\} . We can assume  s s  is of type  a ^ \hat{a} . If  s = x 1 s=x_{1} , then  s s  is common lower bound of  { y 1 , y 2 , y 3 } \{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\} . If  s ≠ x i s\neq x_{i}  for  1 ≤ i ≤ 3 1\leq i\leq 3 , then the argument in the previous paragraph implies that  s s  is adjacent to both  y 1 y_{1}  and  y 2 y_{2} , hence  s s  is a lower bound for  { y 1 , y 2 , y 3 } \{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\} .
It remains to consider the case  s ≠ x 1 , x 2 s\neq x_{1},x_{2}  but  s = x 3 s=x_{3} , and the case  s ≠ x 1 , x 3 s\neq x_{1},x_{3}  but  s = x 2 s=x_{2} . This two cases are symmetric, so we only look at the case  s ≠ x 1 , x 2 s\neq x_{1},x_{2}  but  s = x 3 s=x_{3} . By the argument in the previous paragraph,  s s  is adjacent to  y 2 y_{2} , so  s ≤ y 2 s\leq y_{2} . As  x 3 ≤ y 1 x_{3}\leq y_{1} , we know  s ≤ y 1 s\leq y_{1} . Thus  s s  is common lower bound of  { y 1 , y 2 , y 3 } \{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\} . 
 
 
 If each of  { y 1 , y 2 , y 3 } \{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\}  is of type  b ^ \hat{b} . Let  y i ′ y^{\prime}_{i}  be a vertex of type  c ^ \hat{c}  such that  y i ≤ y i ′ y_{i}\leq y^{\prime}_{i} . Lemma  7.2  gives a vertex  s s  which is a common lower bound of  { y 1 ′ , y 2 ′ , y 3 ′ } \{y^{\prime}_{1},y^{\prime}_{2},y^{\prime}_{3}\} . We can assume  s s  is of type  a ^ \hat{a} . If  s ∉ { x 1 , x 2 , x 3 } s\notin\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\} , as  { x 1 , x 2 , x 3 } \{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\}  is pairwise distinct, then the previous argument implies that  s s  is adjacent to each of  { y 1 , y 2 , y 3 } \{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\} , hence  s s  is a lower bound for them. If  s ∈ { x 1 , x 2 , x 3 } s\in\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\} , then we assume without loss of generality that  s = x 3 s=x_{3} . As  x 3 x_{3}  is a lower bound for  y 3 y_{3}  and  y 1 y_{1} , we know  s ≤ y 1 s\leq y_{1}  and  s ≤ y 3 s\leq y_{3} . As  s ≠ x 1 s\neq x_{1}  and  s ≠ x 2 s\neq x_{2} , the previous argument implies that  s s  is adjacent to  y 2 y_{2} , hence  s ≤ y 2 s\leq y_{2} . Thus  s s  is a lower bound for  { y 1 , y 2 , y 3 } \{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma 7.2 . 
 
 Let  Δ \Delta  be as in Theorem  7.1 .
Take vertices  { x 1 , x 2 , x 3 } ∈ Δ \{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\}\in\Delta  of type  a ^ \hat{a}  and  { y 1 , y 2 , y 3 } ∈ Δ \{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\}\in\Delta  of type  c ^ \hat{c}  such that  x i ≤ y i x_{i}\leq y_{i}  and  x i + 1 ≤ y i x_{i+1}\leq y_{i}  for all  i ∈ ℤ / 3 ​ ℤ i\in\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z} . We assume the edge loop in  Δ \Delta  formed by  ω = y 1 ​ x 1 ​ y 2 ​ x 2 ​ y 3 ​ x 3 ​ y 1 \omega=y_{1}x_{1}y_{2}x_{2}y_{3}x_{3}y_{1}  is a local embedding. Then there is a vertex  s ∈ Δ s\in\Delta  such that  s s  is adjacent to each of  { y 1 , y 2 , y 3 } \{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 From Definition  3.6 , the 6-cycle  ω \omega  gives a word 
 

 
 
 w = w a ​ b ​ w b ​ c ​ w a ​ b ′ ​ w b ​ c ′ ​ w a ​ b ′′ ​ w b ​ c ′′ , w=w_{ab}w_{bc}w^{\prime}_{ab}w^{\prime}_{bc}w^{\prime\prime}_{ab}w^{\prime\prime}_{bc}, 
 
 
 where the subword  w a ​ b w_{ab}  is a word only using  a a  and  b b  (similar constraints applies to other subwords of  w w ). Moreover,  w w  represents the trivial element in  A Λ A_{\Lambda} . Let  Σ \Sigma  be the polyhedron dual to the reflection arrangement of type  H 3 H_{3}  in  ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3} , and  Σ ^ \widehat{\Sigma}  be the associated Salvetti complex.
This word gives a null-homotopic path in  Σ ^ \widehat{\Sigma}  as follows: 
 

 
 
 P = P a ​ b ​ P b ​ c ​ P a ​ b ′ ​ P b ​ c ′ ​ P a ​ b ′′ ​ P b ​ c ′′ . P=P_{ab}P_{bc}P^{\prime}_{ab}P^{\prime}_{bc}P^{\prime\prime}_{ab}P^{\prime\prime}_{bc}. 
 
 
 We denote the sub-segments of this path by  { P i } i = 1 6 \{P_{i}\}_{i=1}^{6} . Let  𝖢 \mathsf{C}  be the Coxeter complex of type  H 3 H_{3} .
Let  π : Δ → 𝖢 \pi:\Delta\to\mathsf{C}  be the map induced by the action of the pure Artin group on  Δ \Delta . 
 
 
 Case 1: the  π \pi -image of the 6-cycle  ω \omega  is a single edge  x ¯ ​ y ¯ \bar{x}\bar{y}  in  𝖢 \mathsf{C} .  Let  C x ¯ C_{\bar{x}}  (resp.  C y ¯ C_{\bar{y}} ) be the 2-cell in  Σ \Sigma  dual to  x ¯ \bar{x}  (resp.  y ¯ \bar{y} ). Let  C ^ x ¯ \widehat{C}_{\bar{x}}  and  C ^ y ¯ \widehat{C}_{\bar{y}}  be the associated standard subcomplexes of  Σ ^ \widehat{\Sigma} . Up to replacing  w = Π i = 1 6 ​ w i w=\Pi_{i=1}^{6}w_{i}  by an equivalent word (in the sense of Definition  3.6 ), we can assume  P i P_{i}  is a loop in  C ^ x ¯ \widehat{C}_{\bar{x}}  (resp.  C ^ y ¯ \widehat{C}_{\bar{y}} ) for  i i  odd (resp. for  i i  even). Let  Z ^ \widehat{Z}  be the complex defined before Lemma  6.5 . Recall from Section  6.1  that there is an embedding  Z ^ → Σ ^ \widehat{Z}\to\widehat{\Sigma} . Thus we can also view  Z ^ \widehat{Z}  as a subcomplex of  Σ ^ \widehat{\Sigma} . Recall that edges of  Σ ^ \widehat{\Sigma}  are oriented and labeled by  { a , b , c } \{a,b,c\}  (Definition  2.11 ), this also gives label and orientation of edges in  Z ^ \widehat{Z}  via the inclusion of  Z ^ \widehat{Z}  into  Σ ^ \widehat{\Sigma} .
Up to a symmetry of  Σ ^ \widehat{\Sigma} , we can assume 
 

 
 
 P ⊂ C ^ x ¯ ∪ C ^ y ¯ ⊂ Z ^ . P\subset\widehat{C}_{\bar{x}}\cup\widehat{C}_{\bar{y}}\subset\widehat{Z}. 
 
 
 Moreover, we can assume  C ^ x ¯ = X ^ 11 \widehat{C}_{\bar{x}}=\widehat{X}_{11}  and  C ^ y ¯ = X ^ 22 \widehat{C}_{\bar{y}}=\widehat{X}_{22} . As we are also viewing  Z ^ \widehat{Z}  as a subcomplex of  X ^ 1 ∪ X ^ 2 \widehat{X}_{1}\cup\widehat{X}_{2} , by Lemma  6.5 ,  P P  is null-homotopic in  X ^ 1 ∪ X ^ 2 \widehat{X}_{1}\cup\widehat{X}_{2} . Assume 
 P 1 , P 3 , P 5 ⊂ X ^ 22 P_{1},P_{3},P_{5}\subset\widehat{X}_{22}  and  P 2 , P 4 , P 6 ⊂ X ^ 11 P_{2},P_{4},P_{6}\subset\widehat{X}_{11} . 
 As  P = ∪ i = 1 6 P i P=\cup_{i=1}^{6}P_{i}  is null-homotopic in  X X , it lifts to a loop  P ~ = ∪ i = 1 6 P ~ i \widetilde{P}=\cup_{i=1}^{6}\widetilde{P}_{i}  in the universal cover  X ~ \widetilde{X}  of  X ^ 1 ∪ X ^ 2 \widehat{X}_{1}\cup\widehat{X}_{2} . A  standard subcomplex  of  X ^ 1 ∪ X ^ 2 \widehat{X}_{1}\cup\widehat{X}_{2}  is an intersection of  { X ^ i ​ j } 1 ≤ i , j ≤ 2 \{\widehat{X}_{ij}\}_{1\leq i,j\leq 2} . A  standard subcomplex  of  X ~ \widetilde{X}  (of type  X ^ i ​ j \widehat{X}_{ij} ) is a lift of  X ^ i ​ j \widehat{X}_{ij}  in  X ~ \widetilde{X} . 
 
 
 Let  𝕌 \mathbb{U}  be the development of the complex of group  𝒰 \mathcal{U} , endowed with the CAT(0) metric as in Section  6.1 . For a vertex  z ∈ 𝕌 z\in\mathbb{U} , denote the subcomplex of  X ~ \widetilde{X}  associated with  z z  by  X ~ z \widetilde{X}_{z} .
The loop  P ~ \widetilde{P}  gives a loop  ω 𝕌 \omega_{\mathbb{U}}  with consecutive vertices denoted by  { z i } i ∈ ℤ / 6 ​ ℤ \{z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}}  in  𝕌 \mathbb{U}  where  X ~ z 1 , X ~ z 3 , X ~ z 5 \widetilde{X}_{z_{1}},\widetilde{X}_{z_{3}},\widetilde{X}_{z_{5}}  (resp.  X ~ z 2 , X ~ z 4 , X ~ z 6 \widetilde{X}_{z_{2}},\widetilde{X}_{z_{4}},\widetilde{X}_{z_{6}} ) correspond to the lifts of  X ^ 22 \widehat{X}_{22}  (resp.  X ^ 11 \widehat{X}_{11} ) in  X ~ \widetilde{X}  that contain  P ~ 1 , P ~ 3 \widetilde{P}_{1},\widetilde{P}_{3}  and  P ~ 5 \widetilde{P}_{5}  respectively (resp.  P ~ 2 , P ~ 4 \widetilde{P}_{2},\widetilde{P}_{4}  and  P ~ 6 \widetilde{P}_{6} ). 
 
 
 Let  𝔻 → 𝕌 \mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{U}  be a minimal area singular disk diagram (cf. Section  2.8 ) for the loop  ω 𝕌 \omega_{\mathbb{U}} . We will slightly abuse notation and use  z i z_{i}  to denote the point in the boundary cycle of  𝔻 \mathbb{D}  mapping to  z i ∈ 𝕌 z_{i}\in\mathbb{U} . The metric on  𝕌 \mathbb{U}  induces a metric on  𝔻 \mathbb{D} , which is CAT ( 0 ) (0) . For three points  x , y , z x,y,z  in a CAT ( 0 ) (0)  space, we use  ∠ y ​ ( x , z ) \angle_{y}(x,z)  to denote the Alexandrov between the geodesic segments  y ​ x ¯ \overline{yx}  and  y ​ z ¯ \overline{yz}  (cf.  [ BH99 , Chapter II.3.1] ). 
 
 
 In  𝕌 \mathbb{U}  we have  ∠ z i ​ ( z i − 1 , z i + 1 ) ≥ π / 2 \angle_{z_{i}}(z_{i-1},z_{i+1})\geq\pi/2  for each  i ∈ ℤ / 6 ​ ℤ i\in\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z} . This is because that  ω \omega  is a local embedding, hence  ω 𝕌 \omega_{\mathbb{U}}  is a local embedding. Thus 
 ∠ z i ​ ( z i − 1 , z i + 1 ) \angle_{z_{i}}(z_{i-1},z_{i+1})  is a multiple of  π / 2 \pi/2  in  𝔻 \mathbb{D} . 
 For each vertex  z i z_{i}  on the boundary cycle  ω 𝕌 \omega_{\mathbb{U}}  of  𝔻 \mathbb{D} , the  interval angle  at  z i z_{i}  is the quantity  α ⁡ ( z i ) \alpha(z_{i})  defined in Section  2.8 .
By the combinatorial Gauss-Bonnet formula in Section  2.8 , least four internal angles of  ω 𝕌 \omega_{\mathbb{U}}  in  𝔻 \mathbb{D}  is  π / 2 \pi/2 . 
 
 
 If at least five internal angles of  ω 𝕌 \omega_{\mathbb{U}}  in  𝔻 \mathbb{D}  is  π / 2 \pi/2 , then  𝔻 \mathbb{D}  has exactly one interior vertex of type  X ^ 12 \widehat{X}_{12}  or  X ^ 21 \widehat{X}_{21} .
We only discuss the case where the internal vertex is of type  X ^ 12 \widehat{X}_{12} , as the other case is similar and easier. Let  T ⊂ X ~ T\subset\widetilde{X}  be the lift of  X ^ 12 \widehat{X}_{12}  corresponding to this internal vertex. Then each  X ~ z i \widetilde{X}_{z_{i}}  has non-empty intersection with  T T . For each  i ∈ ℤ / 6 ​ ℤ i\in\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z} , let  t i t_{i}  be the terminal point of  P ~ i \widetilde{P}_{i}  (which is the starting point of  P ~ i + 1 \widetilde{P}_{i+1} ) and let 
 

 
 
 t i ′ = X ~ z i ∩ X ~ z i + 1 ∩ T . t^{\prime}_{i}=\widetilde{X}_{z_{i}}\cap\widetilde{X}_{z_{i+1}}\cap T. 
 
 
 Such triple intersection is nonempty because they corresponds to three vertices of 2-face in  𝕌 \mathbb{U} .
Set  Q ~ i \widetilde{Q}_{i}  to be an edge path in  T ∩ X ~ z i T\cap\widetilde{X}_{z_{i}}  from  t i − 1 t_{i-1}  to  t i t_{i} . Let  Θ i \Theta_{i}  be a path in  X ~ z i ∩ X ~ z i + 1 \widetilde{X}_{z_{i}}\cap\widetilde{X}_{z_{i+1}}  from  t i t_{i}  to  t i ′ t^{\prime}_{i} . Note that  Q ~ i \widetilde{Q}_{i}  and  Θ i \Theta_{i}  exist by Lemma  6.3 .
Then the loop 
 

 
 
 ( Θ 6 − 1 P ~ 1 Θ 1 ) ( Θ 1 − 1 P ~ 2 Θ 2 ) ⋯ ( Θ 5 − 1 P ~ 6 Θ 6 ) (\Theta^{-1}_{6}\widetilde{P}_{1}\Theta_{1})(\Theta^{-1}_{1}\widetilde{P}_{2}\Theta_{2})\cdots(\Theta^{-1}_{5}\widetilde{P}_{6}\Theta_{6}) 
 
 
 gives a word which is equivalent to  w w . As  Θ i − 1 − 1 ​ P ~ i ​ Θ i \Theta^{-1}_{i-1}\widetilde{P}_{i}\Theta_{i}  is homotopic to  Q ~ i \widetilde{Q}_{i}  rel endpoints in  X ~ z i \widetilde{X}_{z_{i}} , we know the word traced out by  Θ i − 1 − 1 ​ P ~ i ​ Θ i \Theta^{-1}_{i-1}\widetilde{P}_{i}\Theta_{i}  and the word traced out by  Q ~ i \widetilde{Q}_{i}  represent the same element in the group  A a ​ b A_{ab}  (i.e. the subgroup of  A A  generated by  a a  and  b b ) or  A b ​ c A_{bc} . Thus we assume instead that  Q ~ i \widetilde{Q}_{i}  traces out the word  w i w_{i} , and replace  P ~ \widetilde{P}  by  Q ~ \widetilde{Q} , which is the concatenation of all the  Q ~ i \widetilde{Q}_{i} .
As  Q ~ i ⊂ T ∩ X ~ z i \widetilde{Q}_{i}\subset T\cap\widetilde{X}_{z_{i}}  and by Lemma  6.3 ,  T ∩ X ~ z i T\cap\widetilde{X}_{z_{i}}  is a lift of  X ^ 12 ∩ X ^ 11 \widehat{X}_{12}\cap\widehat{X}_{11}  or  X ^ 12 ∩ X ^ 22 \widehat{X}_{12}\cap\widehat{X}_{22} , we deduce that  w i ∈ A a ​ b w_{i}\in A_{ab}  for  i i  odd, and  w i w_{i}  is a power of  c c  for  i i  even. Moreover,  w w  still represents the trivial element in  A Λ A_{\Lambda} . 
 
 
 The new loop  Q ~ \widetilde{Q}  has the advantage that its projection  Q Q  to  Z ^ \widehat{Z}  lies inside a smaller subcomplex, i.e.  Q ~ ⊂ X ^ 12 ∩ ( X ~ 11 ∪ X ~ 22 ) \widetilde{Q}\subset\widehat{X}_{12}\cap(\widetilde{X}_{11}\cup\widetilde{X}_{22}) . By construction,  Q ⊂ Z ^ Q\subset\widehat{Z}  starts and ends at  X ^ 11 ∩ X ^ 12 ∩ X ^ 22 \widehat{X}_{11}\cap\widehat{X}_{12}\cap\widehat{X}_{22} . Note that there is a unique loop  R ⊂ Z ^ R\subset\widehat{Z}  starting and ending at  X ^ 11 ∩ X ^ 21 ∩ X ^ 22 \widehat{X}_{11}\cap\widehat{X}_{21}\cap\widehat{X}_{22}  such that  R R  traces out the same word as  Q Q . As  w w  represents the trivial element in  A Λ A_{\Lambda} , Lemma  6.5  implies that  R R  is null-homotopic in  X ^ 1 ∪ X ^ 2 \widehat{X}_{1}\cup\widehat{X}_{2} . Thus we will work with  R R  instead of  Q Q  now. Note that  R i ⊂ X ^ 22 R_{i}\subset\widehat{X}_{22}  for  i = 1 , 3 , 5 i=1,3,5  and  R i ⊂ X ~ 11 ∩ X ~ 21 R_{i}\subset\widetilde{X}_{11}\cap\widetilde{X}_{21}  for  i = 2 , 4 , 6 i=2,4,6 . 
 
 
 We lift  R R  to be a loop  R ~ ⊂ X ~ \widetilde{R}\subset\widetilde{X} .
Let  T i ′ T^{\prime}_{i}  be the standard subcomplex of type  X ^ 22 \widehat{X}_{22}  (resp.  X ^ 21 \widehat{X}_{21} ) containing  R ~ i \widetilde{R}_{i}  for  i = 1 , 3 , 5 i=1,3,5  (resp.  i = 2 , 4 , 6 i=2,4,6 ). Let  z i ′ ∈ 𝔻 z^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{D}  be the vertex associated with  T i ′ T^{\prime}_{i} . Then  { z i ′ } i = 1 6 \{z^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{6}  forms consecutive vertices in a cycle  ω 𝕌 ′ ⊂ 𝕌 \omega^{\prime}_{\mathbb{U}}\subset\mathbb{U} . As each  w i w_{i}  is a nonzero power of  c c  for  i i  even, we know for  i i  even 
 

 
 
 ∠ z i ′ ​ ( z i − 1 ′ , z i + 1 ′ ) = π . \angle_{z^{\prime}_{i}}(z^{\prime}_{i-1},z^{\prime}_{i+1})=\pi. 
 
 
 So the  ω 𝕌 ′ \omega^{\prime}_{\mathbb{U}}  is a geodesic triangle with vertices at  z 1 ′ , z 3 ′ , z 5 ′ z^{\prime}_{1},z^{\prime}_{3},z^{\prime}_{5}  with three sides having equal length. Moreover,  ∠ z i ′ ​ ( z i − 1 ′ , z i + 1 ′ ) \angle_{z^{\prime}_{i}}(z^{\prime}_{i-1},z^{\prime}_{i+1})  is a multiple of  π / 2 \pi/2 . As  𝕌 \mathbb{U}  is CAT(0), the only possibility is that the triangle  Δ ⁡ ( z 1 ′ , z 3 ′ , z 5 ′ ) \Delta(z^{\prime}_{1},z^{\prime}_{3},z^{\prime}_{5})  is degenerate such that the three sides share a common midpoint, i.e.  z 2 ′ = z 4 ′ = z 6 ′ z^{\prime}_{2}=z^{\prime}_{4}=z^{\prime}_{6} . This means  R ~ 3 \widetilde{R}_{3}  is a path in  T 3 ′ T^{\prime}_{3}  from  T 2 ′ ∩ T 3 ′ T^{\prime}_{2}\cap T^{\prime}_{3}  to  T 4 ′ ∩ T 3 ′ = T 2 ′ ∩ T 3 ′ T^{\prime}_{4}\cap T^{\prime}_{3}=T^{\prime}_{2}\cap T^{\prime}_{3} . Thus  R ~ 3 \widetilde{R}_{3}  is homotopic in  T 3 ′ T^{\prime}_{3}  to a path inside  T 2 ′ ∩ T 3 ′ T^{\prime}_{2}\cap T^{\prime}_{3} , which means  w a ​ b ′ w^{\prime}_{ab}  and  a k a^{k}  represent the same element in  A a ​ b A_{ab} . The same is true for  w a ​ b w_{ab}  and  w a ​ b ′′ w^{\prime\prime}_{ab}  by a similar argument. Thus each  w i w_{i}  is either a power of  a a  or a power of  c c , and the lemma follows.

Thus by replacing  w a ​ b w_{ab}  by another word which represents the same element in  A a ​ b A_{ab} , we can assume  P ~ 1 ⊂ X ~ z 1 ∩ X ~ z 7 \widetilde{P}_{1}\subset\widetilde{X}_{z_{1}}\cap\widetilde{X}_{z_{7}} .  Suppose one of the internal vertices of  𝔻 \mathbb{D} , say  z 8 z_{8} , is type  X ^ 21 \widehat{X}_{21} . Then the above properties of  P ~ \widetilde{P}  implies  w 4 w_{4}  is a power of  c c  and  w 3 w_{3}  is a power of  a a .  w b ​ c = c k 1 ​ b k 2 ​ c k 3 w_{bc}=c^{k_{1}}b^{k_{2}}c^{k_{3}} ,  w b ​ c ′ = c k 4 w^{\prime}_{bc}=c^{k_{4}}  and  w b ​ c ′′ = c k 5 w^{\prime\prime}_{bc}=c^{k_{5}} .

 Case 2: the  π \pi -image of  ω \omega  is two edges  x ¯ ​ y ¯ \bar{x}\bar{y}  and  y ¯ ​ z ¯ \bar{y}\bar{z}  such that  y ¯ \bar{y}  is of type  a ^ \hat{a} .  Then  x ¯ \bar{x}  and  z ¯ \bar{z}  is of type  c ^ \hat{c} .  which represents the trivial element in the group. Thus

 Moreover, we can assume

 For we assume  k 1 ≠ 0 , k 5 ≠ 0 k_{1}\neq 0,k_{5}\neq 0  and  k 4 ≠ 0 k_{4}\neq 0 .

Since 
 k 1 ≠ 0 k_{1}\neq 0 ,  k 5 ≠ 0 k_{5}\neq 0  and  k 4 ≠ 0 k_{4}\neq 0 ,

 so the same holds in  𝔻 \mathbb{D} . 
 
 1.  
 
 If  ∠ z 2 ​ ( z 1 , z 31 ) ≥ 3 ​ π / 4 \angle_{z_{2}}(z_{1},z_{31})\geq 3\pi/4  and  ∠ z 4 ​ ( z 5 , z 33 ) ≥ 3 ​ π / 4 \angle_{z_{4}}(z_{5},z_{33})\geq 3\pi/4  in  𝔻 \mathbb{D} , then from the Gauss-Bonnet formula ( 2.16 ) we know all the other angles at the vertices of  ω 𝕌 \omega_{\mathbb{U}}  in  𝔻 \mathbb{D}  has to be  π / 2 \pi/2 . As  𝔻 \mathbb{D}  is made of right-angled isosceles triangles, by consider the sequence of right-angled turns in the edge path  z 4 → z 5 → z 6 → z 1 → z 2 z_{4}\to z_{5}\to z_{6}\to z_{1}\to z_{2} , we must have  z 2 = z 4 z_{2}=z_{4} , which is impossible as  z 2 → z 31 → z 32 → z 33 → z 4 z_{2}\to z_{31}\to z_{32}\to z_{33}\to z_{4}  is a geodesic in  𝕌 \mathbb{U} .

Thus  z 1 z_{1}  is adjacent to  z 32 z_{32} . Now we are reduced to consider a new cycle  ω 𝕌 ′ \omega^{\prime}_{\mathbb{U}}  of form  z 1 → z 32 → z 33 → z 4 → z 5 → z 6 → z 1 z_{1}\to z_{32}\to z_{33}\to z_{4}\to z_{5}\to z_{6}\to z_{1}  and a disk diagram  𝔻 ′ ⊂ 𝔻 \mathbb{D}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{D}  bounded by  ω 𝕌 ′ \omega^{\prime}_{\mathbb{U}} . Note that  ∠ z 32 ​ ( z 1 , z 33 ) = 3 ​ π / 4 \angle_{z_{32}}(z_{1},z_{33})=3\pi/4  in  𝔻 ′ \mathbb{D}^{\prime} . Thus by Gauss-Bonnet formula, all other angles at the vertices of  ω 𝕌 ′ \omega^{\prime}_{\mathbb{U}}  in  𝔻 ′ \mathbb{D}^{\prime}  is  π / 2 \pi/2 . We deduce as before that  z 32 = z 4 z_{32}=z_{4} , which is impossible as  z 32 → z 33 → z 4 z_{32}\to z_{33}\to z_{4}  is a geodesic in  𝕌 \mathbb{U} .

From the description of  P 1 P_{1}  and  P 4 P_{4} , we know  c ^ \hat{c}  does not collapse any edges in  P 1 P_{1}  and  P 4 P_{4} . Thus the loop  Q 1 Q_{1}  represents an element in  π 1 ​ ( C ^ 1 ′ ) \pi_{1}(\widehat{C}^{\prime}_{1})  which is not contained in  π 1 ​ ( C ^ 1 ′ ∩ C ^ 2 ′ ) \pi_{1}(\widehat{C}^{\prime}_{1}\cap\widehat{C}^{\prime}_{2}) , which implies  x 1 ′ ≠ x 3 ′ x^{\prime}_{1}\neq x^{\prime}_{3} . Similarly  y 2 ′ ≠ y 3 ′ y^{\prime}_{2}\neq y^{\prime}_{3} .

 Claim 7.7 . 
 
 Let  { x i } \{x_{i}\}  and  { y i } \{y_{i}\}  be as in the lemma. Let  y 2 ′ ≠ y 2 y^{\prime}_{2}\neq y_{2}  be a vertex of type  c ^ \hat{c}  in  Δ \Delta  which is adjacent to both  x 1 x_{1}  and  x 2 x_{2} . Suppose there is a vertex  s ′ ∈ Δ s^{\prime}\in\Delta  such that  s ′ s^{\prime}  is adjacent to each of  { y 1 , y 2 ′ , y 3 } \{y_{1},y^{\prime}_{2},y_{3}\} . Then  s ′ s^{\prime}  is adjacent to each of  { y 1 , y 2 , y 3 } \{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 First we consider the case  s ′ ≠ x 1 s^{\prime}\neq x_{1}  and  s ′ ≠ x 2 s^{\prime}\neq x_{2} . By Theorem  3.17 , there are vertices  q 1 , q 2 , q 3 q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}  of type  b ^ \hat{b}  such that 
 
 •  
 
 q 1 q_{1}  is adjacent to each of  y 1 , x 1 , y 2 ′ , s ′ y_{1},x_{1},y^{\prime}_{2},s^{\prime} ; 
 
 •  
 
 q 2 q_{2}  is adjacent to each of  y 2 ′ , x 2 , y 3 , s ′ y^{\prime}_{2},x_{2},y_{3},s^{\prime} ; 
 
 •  
 
 q 3 q_{3}  is adjacent to each of  x 1 , y 2 , x 2 , y 2 ′ x_{1},y_{2},x_{2},y^{\prime}_{2} . 
 
 
 Then  s ′ ​ q 1 ​ x 1 ​ q 3 ​ x 2 ​ q 2 s^{\prime}q_{1}x_{1}q_{3}x_{2}q_{2}  form a 6-cycle in  lk ⁡ ( y 2 ′ , Δ ) \operatorname{lk}(y^{\prime}_{2},\Delta) . However, as  l ​ k ​ ( y 2 ′ , Δ ) lk(y^{\prime}_{2},\Delta)  is a copy of the Artin complex of  A a ​ b A_{ab} , which has girth  ≥ 10 \geq 10  by  [ AS83 , Lemma 6] . Thus the 6-cycle is degenerate. As  s ′ ≠ x 1 s^{\prime}\neq x_{1} ,  s ′ ≠ x 2 s^{\prime}\neq x_{2}  and  x 1 ≠ x 2 x_{1}\neq x_{2} , we know the back-tracking vertices of this 6-cycle is a subset of  { x 1 , x 2 , s ′ } \{x_{1},x_{2},s^{\prime}\} , which is impossible.
Thus either  s ′ = x 1 s^{\prime}=x_{1}  or  s ′ = x 2 s^{\prime}=x_{2} , and the claim follows.
∎

 ω ′ = y 1 ′ ​ x 1 ′ ​ y 2 ′ ​ x 2 ′ ​ y 3 ′ ​ x 3 ′ \omega^{\prime}=y^{\prime}_{1}x^{\prime}_{1}y^{\prime}_{2}x^{\prime}_{2}y^{\prime}_{3}x^{\prime}_{3}

 Q ~ 35 ′ ⊂ V ~ 4 ∩ V ~ 3 \widetilde{Q}^{\prime}_{35}\subset\widetilde{V}_{4}\cap\widetilde{V}_{3}  from  q 3 q_{3}  to the endpoint of  Q ~ 3 \widetilde{Q}_{3} . 
 
 
 We can assume each  Q ~ 3 ​ i ′ \widetilde{Q}^{\prime}_{3i}  is a geodesic path.  Thus 
 

 
 
 Q 3 ′ ⊂ V 3 ∩ ( V w 1 ∪ V z ∪ V w 2 ) . Q^{\prime}_{3}\subset V_{3}\cap(V_{w_{1}}\cup V_{z}\cup V_{w_{2}}). 
 
 
 As  Q 3 = e ^ ​ ( P 3 ) Q_{3}=\hat{e}(P_{3}) , the form of  P 3 P_{3}  implies that 
 Q 3 ⊂ V 3 ∩ ( V w 1 ∪ V z ∪ V w 2 ) Q_{3}\subset V_{3}\cap(V_{w_{1}}\cup V_{z}\cup V_{w_{2}}) .

 V 3 ∩ ( V w 1 ∪ V z ∪ V w 2 ) V_{3}\cap(V_{w_{1}}\cup V_{z}\cup V_{w_{2}})  is a union of three circles.

 which can not be null-homotopic in this graph. Thus the segments  Q 31 Q_{31}  and  Q 35 Q_{35}  must be trivial, implying the claim. By a similarly argument,  Q ~ i \widetilde{Q}_{i}  starts at  q i − 1 q_{i-1}  and ends at  q i q_{i}  for  i = 1 , 3 , 5 i=1,3,5 .  In particular  z 1 , z 21 , z 6 z_{1},z_{21},z_{6}  form a triangle in  𝕌 \mathbb{U} . Hence  T 1 ∩ T 6 ∩ T 21 ≠ ∅ T_{1}\cap T_{6}\cap T_{21}\neq\emptyset .  ( T 1 ∩ T 6 ) ∪ ( T 1 ∩ T 21 ) (T_{1}\cap T_{6})\cup(T_{1}\cap T_{21}) 
 
 
 and passes through  T 1 ∩ T 6 ∩ T 21 T_{1}\cap T_{6}\cap T_{21} . Thus  w a ​ b = b ∗ ​ a ∗ w_{ab}=b^{*}a^{*}  in  A a ​ b A_{ab} . Now the word  w w  becomes 
 

 
 
 a ∗ ⋅ c ∗ ​ b ∗ ​ c ∗ ⋅ a ∗ ​ b ∗ ​ a ∗ ⋅ c ∗ ⋅ w a ​ b ′′ ​ w b ​ c ′′ = 1 . a^{*}\cdot c^{*}b^{*}c^{*}\cdot a^{*}b^{*}a^{*}\cdot c^{*}\cdot w^{\prime\prime}_{ab}w^{\prime\prime}_{bc}=1. 
 
 
 We assume  w w  starts with  a k 1 ⋅ c k 2 a^{k_{1}}\cdot c^{k_{2}} . The new word  w w  takes form: 
 

 
 
 a ∗ ⋅ b ∗ ​ c ∗ ⋅ a ∗ ​ b ∗ ​ a ∗ ⋅ c ∗ ​ w a ​ b ′′ ​ w b ​ c ′′ = 1 . a^{*}\cdot b^{*}c^{*}\cdot a^{*}b^{*}a^{*}\cdot c^{*}w^{\prime\prime}_{ab}w^{\prime\prime}_{bc}=1. 
 
 
 On the level of  ω \omega , this has the effect of replace  y 1 y_{1}  by a vertex  y 1 ′ y^{\prime}_{1}  of type  c ^ \hat{c}  that are adjacent to both  x 1 x_{1}  and  x 3 x_{3} . By the way we handling the word ( 7.3 ), we know  { y 1 ′ , y 3 , y 5 } \{y^{\prime}_{1},y_{3},y_{5}\}  is adjacent to a common vertex in  Δ \Delta .
Then we are done by Claim  7.7 . 
 
 
 Case 7: the  π \pi -image of  ω \omega  is five edges.  Then  π ⁡ ( ω ) \pi(\omega)  is a 4-cycle with an extra edge.
